[KIMBALL JUNCTION WEST] Architectural Design Standards11-18-2025
 AO Architects
DAKOTA PACIFIC REAL ESTATE
1.0 INTRODUCTION
Situated in the Snyderville Basin, a major gateway to the Park City Region, [Kimball Junction West] is an 59 acre district located 
near Kimball Junction and the Utah Olympic Park. Park City Junction is a mixed-use neighborhood designed to prioritize both 
people and places. It promotes pedestrian-oriented development, offers a range of housing options—from market-rate to 
affordable—and encourages walking, biking, and transit use. The neighborhood is designed to connect thoughtfully with adjacent 
communities and nearby natural amenities.
1.1  STANDARDS AND GUIDELINES
The objective of the [Kimball Junction West]  Design Standards is to create a warm, cohesive visual identity across the 
master-planned, mixed-use development. The mix of historical mountain village architecture combined with a 
contemporary palette of materials and simple building forms will create a setting that celebrates the regions rich history 
as well as its future.  Lower scale buildings reflect the areas of the mountain village scale while the larger massing of the 
commercial buildings will favor and gesture more toward a contemporary interpretation of the historical heritage of 
Park City.  Architectural styles featured within these guidelines are intended to deliver an architectural direction that is 
sensitive to the sites rich cultural aesthetics while offering contemporary design elements all while fostering a distinctive 
sense of place not only for today but the future. Additionally, the guidelines are crafted to set a unified design and signage 
language throughout the various districts within [Kimball Junction West].
The [Kimball Junction West]  Design Standards aim to establish uniformity in site organization, architectural style, 
landscape design, and signage strategies.
These guidelines will:
• Ensure the establishment of well-defined pedestrian paths, trails, and bikeways, fostering improved pedestrian   
 movement and connectivity between districts.
• Introduce varied building massing to distinctly shape the overall building elevation aesthetic.
• Apply details that are proportionate to each building’s scale and district, addressing all facades.
• Encourage diverse architectural expressions that align with the unified design themes across buildings of different  
 sizes and uses.
• Organize buildings, traffic flow, parking, and service zones in a manner that bolsters both the identity and   
 functionality of the development while ensuring architectural coherence.
• Design access points to accommodate the safe movement of both vehicles and pedestrians.
• Generate a unique yet harmonious architectural presence that echoes the distinct functions of each building,   
 aligning with the design principles of other regional master-planned projects.
• Create a cohesive landscaping concept that complements the site plan, enhances entry points, provides shade   
 and screening in parking areas, and ensures consistency along the streetscape.
• Implement consistent exterior lighting to ensure safety and establish a distinct ambiance in the nighttime   
 environment, mindful of minimizing disruption to adjacent areas and future developments.
• Develop a comprehensive and imaginative approach to signage and wayfinding.
• Craft an overarching signage scheme that connects all the individual districts within a classic modern design   
 framework.
•  Provide images solely meant to only demonstrate specific design principles. They are illustrative in nature and   
 should  not be interpreted as standards, guidelines, or requirements.
• Provide requirements that will be used in conjuctiion with the Development Agreement dated 7-28-25.

[KIMBALL JUNCTION WEST] Architectural Design Standards11-18-2025
 AO Architects
DAKOTA PACIFIC REAL ESTATE
A mixed-use, master-planned district that aligns with Kimball Junction’s vision: walkable and connected, with a strong civic heart 
for community gathering and events; people-oriented streets and paths; transit integration and future connections; consolidated 
parking to free the public realm; varied housing choices; high visual quality; and a sustainable, adaptable built environment.
2.1  DISTRICT CHARACTERISTICS
2.1.1 Mixed-Use Neighborhood - Livability and Character
Create a true “place,” not a collection of projects—homes, shops, services, civic uses, and workplaces stitched 
together by streets, plazas, and trails. This combination will promote efficiencies in people, services, mobility, 
and infrastructure. These are places where residential, retail, service, and civic uses are connected by a network 
of public spaces. Mixed-use neighborhoods concentrate on active ground floors, layered uses vertically and 
pedestrian connections to parking and transit. Mixed-Use neighborhoods create a greater sense of community. 
2.1.2 Walkable Neighborhood / People-Oriented Environment
Walkable neighborhoods foster diversity—of people, uses, and experiences. They are easier to move to, though, 
and around. Streets and public spaces are designed for people first, with vehicles accommodated but not 
prioritized. The five-minute walk becomes the default trip, supported by seamless connections to, though, and 
across the district. Parcels are stitched to plazas, transit, and regional trails with short, direct routes that encourage 
everyday movement and interaction. 
2.1.3 Housing Diversity / Housing Choices
Offer a visible spectrum of homes—including workforce and attainable options—within walking distance of daily 
needs and transit. Provide a balanced mix of unit sizes and types across phases, ensuring every building has a clear 
street address and a welcoming entry. Integrate housing near services, transit, and centralized parking to reduce 
car dependence and strengthen neighborhood connectivity. 
2.1.4 Massing & Scale
Keep buildings visually calm and human-scale while accommodating program needs. Maintain quiet, parapeted 
rooflines with equipment fully concealed; avoid expressive roof forms that dominate the skyline. Rely on massing, 
proportion, and scale—rather than applied ornament—to achieve architectural character.
2.1.5 Parks and Open Space
Parks and open space should foster a tranquil atmosphere for relaxation and community engagement. Trees, 
planting, and comfortable street furniture enhance the quality and enjoyment of these spaces. Given the 
challenges of a mountain climate, shade & wind reduction features should be strategically placed to extend 
usability throughout the year.
2.0 DESIGN PRINCIPLES

2.0 DESIGN PRINCIPLES
2.2  CIRCULATION & CONNECTIVITY 
2.2.1 Streets
Tech Center Drive and supporting roads are multi-functional thoroughfares that extend beyond just vehicular 
traffic; it also supports pedestrian activity, access to mass transit, electric vehicle parking and bicycle lanes. Streets 
are enriched by social activities such as walking, outdoor dining, and shopping experiences. Elements such as 
greenery, street furniture, lighting, public art, and signage are essential in shaping a distinctive and successful 
streetscape. Streets and pedestrian connections should be designed as vibrant, people-oriented places.
2.2.2 Pedestrian Trail System
Utilize both street adjacent off-street paths that are accessible, safe, and enjoyable. These paths should feature 
durable, all-season surfaces (for instance: concrete, asphalt, crushed granite) and provide direct links to the 
plaza, transit, and surrounding neighborhoods. Designed to accommodate both walking and biking, trails should 
extend into nearby natural resources and amenities. The internal trail system will connect the P3 Project and the 
Residential District to the pedestrian bridge, creating connections between landmarks and destinations within 
the Kimball Junction Neighborhood without creating specific public trailheads with associated parking and 
requirements. 
2.2.3 Bridge
A new pedestrian bridge across SR-224 is planned to connect the east and west neighborhoods of Kimball 
Junction, strengthen walkability and unify the district. The Utah Department of Transportation (UDOT) will 
construct the bridge at the P3 site or deliver an alternative pedestrian crossing along SR-224, ensuring safe and 
direct connections throughout the neighborhood. 
2.2.4 Transit Station & Transit Routes
A new bus transit station on the south side of the site will connect the community to nearby neighborhoods 
and regional destinations. With safe boarding areas, rider amenities, and access to e-bikes, EV charging, and 
other sustainable options, it will make getting around easier and greener. The station will reduce traffic while 
strengthening connectivity across the community.

DAKOTA PACIFIC REAL ESTATE
2.0 DESIGN PRINCIPLES
2.3  TOPOGRAPHY
2.3.1 Slopes & Terrain
Shape buildings and public spaces to follow existing grades, using stepped forms and terraces. Step massing with 
the slopes to avoid tall, exposed foundations or retaining walls. Integrate grade transitions into the architectural 
and landscape design, composing terraces, stairs, and planters as cohesive elements while carefully managing 
drainage and snow melt.  
2.3.2 Cut and Fill
Balance earthwork where practical to preserve existing soils, trees, grasses, and landscaping, while minimizing the 
need for imported or exported fill.
2.4  SUSTAINABLE COMMUNITY & SMART GROWTH
A sustainable community is a key component of [Kimball Junction West]. Smart growth shall be achieved through 
compact development that minimizes travel distances and supports low-carbon mobility through transit options, 
and EV infrastructure. Construction shall follow sustainable, low-waste practices, including water conservation 
measures, with the goal of delivering high-performing, sustainably designed buildings. Electric vehicle charging 
stations shall be provided throughout the P3 and Residential districts.  EV charging within residential areas shall 
provide  "Level 1” charging and  "Level 2 “  in guest parking areas. EV charging within P3 areas shall be "Level 2”  
charging.

DAKOTA PACIFIC REAL ESTATE
3.0 DISTRICT PLANS
3.0.1 P3 District 
At the heart of the [Kimball Junction West] Master Plan is the P3 District, which includes a transit center, a 
structured parking facility, a civic plaza, and a public commons. These are framed by mixed-use residential and 
commercial buildings and are linked to the eastern neighborhoods of Kimball Junction by a grade-separated 
pedestrian bridge spanning Highway 224.
The P3 District also features a flexible civic green with views toward the Utah Olympic Park. Designed as an 
event-ready public commons, this green accommodates festivals, community programs, informal recreation, and 
everyday gathering.
A site and utilities are reserved for a future civic building fronting the park, allowing a seamless relationship 
between the library, the civic green, and surrounding active ground floors.
The P3 Transit Center and Parking Structure function as the primary gateway to the district, supporting car-light, 
pedestrian-oriented activity with convenient intermodal transfers.
To support long-term mobility, the plan reasonably provides for a future gondola, reserving alignment and 
structural clearances for a potential terminal and gondola cableway connection through the plaza area.
3.0.2 Residential District
The Residential District serves as a vital and vibrant population center serving a wide variety of needs within a 
diverse range of housing types and tenures These include workforce housing, multifamily apartments, senior living 
facilities, townhomes, and market-rate residences.
The district will be designed with an emphasis on livability, incorporating pocket parks, open spaces, and safe, 
accessible streets. A network of trails will connect residents to nearby natural areas, residential neighborhoods, and 
business communities, encouraging walking and strengthening community connectivity.

4.1  MASSING AND BUILDING HEIGHTS 
4.1.1 Building Massing
Compose buildings as legible volumes with a calm base–middle–top. Along Primary Streets and the plaza, ground 
floors align closely to the walk and are scaled for active use. Corners are simple and open, and façades read as 
ordered bays with depth from recesses, fins, and soffits.
4.1.2 Massing with Grade
Where terrain changes across a building pad, step the volumes and, where appropriate, break the form so both 
uphill and downhill façades remain within allowed height and read human-scaled. Keep primary entries close to 
sidewalk elevation; align floor plates to reduce tall exposed foundations. Where Level 1 sits above the walk, finish 
the base as intentional architecture and limit any exposed foundation per these Architectural Design Standards. 
Coordinate the step with unit depth and maintain façade continuity, drainage, and snow management across the 
transition.
4.1.3 Heights
In the P3 podium, retail floors provide 15–25 ft floor-to-deck for visibility and flexibility. Residential buildings may 
rise to 4 stories or 48 ft above the plaza deck (inclusive of any ground-floor retail where applicable); office buildings 
may rise to 3 stories or 48 ft above the plaza deck. Along SR-224, provide a 15-ft step-back from the podium edge 
at or below 25 ft above the plaza deck (railings excluded). The parking structure is no more than 2 stories above the 
lowest final grade, reading largely subterranean at the northwest and becoming more exposed as grade drops east 
and south.
Outside the podium, multifamily attached buildings may rise to 4 stories and 45 ft from adjacent finished grade, 
and up to 50 ft where one level is a concrete parking story; single-family attached may rise to 3 stories or 35 ft. 
Double-loaded residential buildings may step one level to keep both uphill and downhill façades within the 45-ft 
method; the step should occur at unit depth and be ≥25 ft long.  Where grade falls, up to 5 ft of finished exposed 
foundation may be excluded from height; limit exposure to ≤30% of any side and finish it as part of the façade 
system. 
Rooftop appurtenances may exceed the maximum height by limited amounts when fully screened or enclosed—
e.g., antennas (~5 ft), mechanical screens (~8 ft), elevator penthouse (~8 ft), stair penthouse (~10 ft), modest 
sloped roof allowances where permitted, and small roof-level access or shade elements—all per the controlling 
exhibit per Development Agreement dated 7-28-25. Equipment shall not be visible from streets or the plaza.

4.2  STREETS, SIDEWALKS & ROW 
4.2.1 Streetscape
Design streets as comfortable, low-speed environments that support year-round walking. Coordinate trees, poles, 
furnishings, signs, and snow storage so the pedestrian clear zone remains continuous and uncluttered. 
4.2.2 Sidewalks
Provide continuous sidewalks on both sides of streets, widening at entries, corners, transit stops, and high-activity 
frontages to accommodate gathering and winter maintenance. 
4.2.3 Bike Lanes
Where bikeways are planned, maintain a continuous, visible network with separation or clear markings, protected 
conflict points at intersections, and direct links to long-term secure parking in buildings and abundant short-term 
racks near entries.
4.3  BUILDING SETBACKS 
4.3.1 Front Yard Setback, Side Yard Setback, Rear Yard Setback
Observe corridor buffers established by the approvals: along SR-224 maintain a minimum 100-ft setback from 
the right-of-way; along Ute and Landmark maintain a minimum 25-ft setback from property lines in the podium 
area. At Primary Streetscapes, the maximum setback from the pedestrian walkway is 10 ft (limited exceptions 
for institutional/program needs); for other roadway edges not identified as Primary Streetscapes, the minimum 
setback from property line is 10 ft. Preserve a setback on the north side of Tech Center Drive sufficient to 
accommodate a potential future gondola alignment.  Setbacks for parking against non-residential neighboring 
properties are 5’.  There is no parking setback required between parcels within the project.  However, there shall 
generally be at least 10’ between parking and buildings.
4.3.2 Canopies & Building Projection
Canopies, whether cantilevered or suspended over sidewalks or entries, may project up to 9 feet beyond the 
building facade and must use materials, profiles, and detailing consistent with the building’s architectural 
expression.
Balconies and other upper-level floor projections are permitted only above street level. Individual or combined 
projections may extend up to 6 feet beyond the build-to line, with their total projected length limited to no more 
4.0 DEVELOPMENT STANDARDS

[KIMBALL JUNCTION WEST] Architectural Design Standards11-18-2025
 AO Architects
DAKOTA PACIFIC REAL ESTATE
4.4  OPEN SPACE 
4.4.1 Plaza Alignment & Future Connections
The central plaza is the district’s civic room and shall be aligned to connect to the planned pedestrian bridge over 
SR-224; its structure and clearances shall accommodate a potential gondola terminal and east–west cable run 
through the plaza. 
4.4.2 Improved Areas
Design the plaza and pocket spaces for all-season use: winter sun access, wind moderation, summer shade, 
durable seating, event power and water, and discreet drainage. Line active edges with transparent frontages and 
doors that open directly to the space. 
4.4.3 Native Areas
At edges and slopes, use native/adapted plant communities and durable surfaces sized for freeze–thaw and snow 
storage; coordinate irrigation, soil volumes, and maintenance access for long-term health.
4.4.4 Amphitheater Park
A tiered lawn amphitheater sized for community events, targeting about 800 people with bring-your-own chairs 
and blankets. Terraces provide clear sight lines with accessible routes to each tier, and a small hardstand supports 
temporary stages. Power, data, and A/V may be built in for performances. The design should consider year-round 
uses and activation, including winter.  For instance, small snow rail features will attract youth to the area during the 
snowy season. Other year-round opportunities may include a children’s play zone, interactive art, and picnic lawns 
and tables.
4.0 DEVELOPMENT STANDARDS

[KIMBALL JUNCTION WEST] Architectural Design Standards11-18-2025
 AO Architects
DAKOTA PACIFIC REAL ESTATE
4.5 CIRCULATION SPACE
4.5.1 Access
Organize access hierarchies so service and parking use internal drives and courts, minimizing interruptions to 
primary pedestrian routes and frontages.  
4.5.2 Street Network
Keep blocks permeable with short, legible connections; at key nodes, shape geometry and controls to favor 
pedestrian crossings and reliable bus operations.
4.5.3 Service Access
Locate loading, refuse, and utilities off primary streets; route movements through internal courts; contain queuing 
within buildings; screen all service functions (see Screening).
4.5.4 Pedestrian and Bicycle Network
Keep blocks permeable with short, legible connections; at key nodes, shape geometry and controls to favor 
pedestrian crossings and reliable bus operations.
4.5.5 Trails
Deliver internal trail links to the SR-224 underpass or pedestrian bridge and to adjacent retail and neighborhoods, 
with accessible slopes, durable surfaces, and wayfinding at forks.  Where feasible, trail alignments will remain 
straight, with integrated speed-calming strategies applied along extended linear segments rather than using 
meandering trail layouts.
4.5.6 Bus Service
Where service plans require, incorporate bus pull-outs, shelters, and—if reasonably necessary—transit lanes along 
Landmark and internal roads, coordinated to avoid pedestrian conflicts. 
4.6  UTILITIES 
4.6.1 Storm Water Management
Integrate a hierarchy of green and gray systems sized for alpine conditions: pretreatment, infiltration or detention, 
controlled discharge, and defined maintenance access. Coordinate with grading, snow-melt paths, and planting 
soil volumes so winter operations do not undermine performance. Where public infrastructure is required but not 
yet built at Final Site Plan or plat, execute a Development Improvements Agreement (DIA) per the controlling 
approvals. 
4.0 DEVELOPMENT STANDARDS
DRAFT - V4
16
15
[KIMBALL JUNCTION WEST] Architectural Design Standards11-18-2025
 AO Architects
DAKOTA PACIFIC REAL ESTATE
4.7 SCREENING
4.7.1 Service Access, Loading, Trash
Internalize loading and refuse rooms where feasible; otherwise, fully screen within architectural enclosures and 
gates integrated with the façade. Locate access from internal drives or alleys; avoid primary street frontages. 
4.7.2 Outdoor Storage, Refuse Collection and Equipment Areas
Prohibit outdoor storage on primary edges. Place all rooftop equipment within parapet screens or integrated 
enclosures consistent with height allowances; equipment and screens shall not be visible from streets or the plaza. 
4.8  PARKING 
4.8.1 On Street
Use on-street spaces to support short-stay access to retail and civic uses without compromising pedestrian 
priority. Mark and manage spaces to preserve clear sightlines at crossings and driveways; pair with bike parking 
and loading zones as needed. 
4.8.2	 Off 	Street
Prioritize structured parking in the P3 podium; commercial and residential located on or above the structure shall 
park within it. Residential minimums are 1 stall per studio/1-bed and 2 stalls per 2-bed+; deed-restricted units at 
≤80% AMI within 0.3 miles of the transit center may reduce by 0.25 (studio/1-bed) and 0.50 (2-bed+). Commercial 
maximums are 5/1,000 sf (medical office) and 4/1,000 sf (office/retail); structured parking may exceed when 
justified, and restaurants/specialty retail may seek additional supply at Final Site Plan. With County Manager 
approval, minimums may be reduced using a shared-parking analysis and traffic demand management that right 
sizes supply. 
4.0 DEVELOPMENT STANDARDS

[KIMBALL JUNCTION WEST] Architectural Design Standards11-18-2025
 AO Architects
DAKOTA PACIFIC REAL ESTATE
5.1 P3 DISTRICT
Building Design 
Buildings that frame the plaza and Tech Center Drive present calm, rectilinear volumes with generous, well-shaded 
glazing at active edges and disciplined, durable wall systems elsewhere. Detailing is precise, with clear joints, 
returns, and soffits suited to freeze–thaw, snow load, and high-alpine sun. Roofs are quiet parapet or low slope lines 
that fully conceal equipment; ground floors feel open and welcoming to the public realm.  
5.1.1 Materials
Use a restrained, climate-fit palette that reads refined rather than rustic. Primary cladding may include 
architectural concrete and precast, masonry or brick, fiber-cement or high-pressure laminate panels, and metal 
plate or composite systems, all detailed with true returns at edges and openings. Reserve natural or engineered 
wood for protected soffits and interior applications, and avoid exterior assemblies that perform poorly in snow and 
strong UV. Where height rules permit exposed foundation, finish it to the same standard as the facade, with no 
unfinished CMU on public frontages.
Avoid historic ornament, thin clip-on stone at exposed corners, vinyl siding, and imitative textures that conflict with 
the district character. Use regionally appropriate stone sparingly as a base or threshold element, and detail it with 
drip edges and thermal breaks to manage water and freeze-thaw.
5.1.2 Glass
Provide generous transparency along the plaza and Primary Street frontages to support visibility and street life, 
then temper openness on upper levels for comfort and energy performance. Select glazing that manages heat gain 
and loss without mirror-like finishes, and keep visible reflectance low so facades remain clear and welcoming rather 
than reflective.
Control glare and solar exposure with architectural means such as deep recesses, exterior shading, and integrated 
fins so daylighting reads as part of the design. Use clear glass at entries and display zones for true color rendition, 
and organize mullions to reinforce the facade’s rhythm and order.
5.0 ARCHITECTURE DESIGN CRITERIA
DRAFT - V4
18
17
[KIMBALL JUNCTION WEST] Architectural Design Standards11-18-2025
 AO Architects
DAKOTA PACIFIC REAL ESTATE
5.1 P3 DISTRICT
5.1.3 Building Colors
 Use of a quiet, desaturated palette that lets form, proportion, and shadow do the work. Favor families of related  
 tones rather than a single scheme, with the field colors drawn from muted neutrals and mineral hues that sit well  
 with sandstone, timber, and metal.  The color palette illustrates a range of related tones but does not    
 establish rules for color placement or mandate specific colors.  Colors identified to be considered as guidelines   
 and not as building standard.  Reserve stronger contrasts for moments of emphasis such as entries, canopies,   
 and key details, and keep saturated color to limited areas rather than large fields. Avoid high-chroma hues and   
 high-gloss finishes on primary facades. Coordinate storefront and curtain wall framing with adjacent cladding so  
 the assembly reads coherent. Calibrate selections to snow and winter light, and choose finishes proven to resist UV  
 and de-icing salts.  
5.1.4 Building & Facade Articulation
Express order, depth, and legibility at the pedestrian scale. No specific facade breaks in length or depth are 
required.  Organize facades as clear bays with consistent heads and sill lines to create architectural massing 
cadence accented offsets and balconies. Position public entries on street or plaza fronts, and where a shared 
entry is not feasible keep the frontage active with doors and generous glazing. A combination of roofline 
variations and facade articulation is encouraged, especially when both occur at the same location rather than 
stipulated dimensional spacing. Articulate building corners with openness and minimal joints and/or material 
reveals, avoiding arbitrary shifts in plane or material that disrupt continuity.  Architectural lighting can be used as a 
secondary layer to underscore articulation without glare. Fixtures should highlight and frame entries, reveals, and 
soffits while preserving comfortable luminance for people on the sidewalk.
5.1.5 Roofs & Overhangs
Sloped roofs with a pitch may extend up to twelve feet (12’-0”) above the allowed Maximum Building Height for 
all parcels. Steeper roof pitches are meant to be used on smaller scale buildings. Use flat or sloped roofs with 
parapet sized to fully conceal all rooftop equipment and any associated screens so rooftop equipment is not visible 
from adjacent streets or the plaza.  Any allowed building appurtenances shall remain within the applicable height 
exceptions, including mechanical screens, elevator overruns, stair penthouses, and similar elements.
Design snow and water management as part of the architecture. Integrate snow retention, primary and overflow 
drainage, and scupper locations into the composition, avoiding exposed or ad hoc measures on prominent 
edges. Coordinate PV readiness with the roof layout by reserving clear zones, pathways, and structural capacity, 
and align these with parapet heights and equipment placement.  No specific roof eave dimension overhang is 
required under these design standards but snow and moisture run off shall be considered and match the adjacent 
residential planning area.
Roof colors are encouraged to harmonize with their surroundings and blend where appropriate. Light and white 
roofs that enhance thermal performance are allowed. Rooftop equipment should not significantly contrast with the 
roof color.
5.0 ARCHITECTURE DESIGN CRITERIA

[KIMBALL JUNCTION WEST] Architectural Design Standards11-18-2025

DAKOTA PACIFIC REAL ESTATE
5.0 ARCHITECTURE DESIGN CRITERIA
5.2 RESIDENTIAL DISTRICT
Building Design 
Neighborhood buildings share the district’s refined, contemporary language at a more intimate scale. Entries are 
obvious and weather-capable; transparency is calibrated for privacy; massing steps with grade so street fronts feel 
comfortable to walk along in all seasons.
5.2.1 Materials
Select durable systems that age well and can be maintained by residents and HOAs. Primary cladding may include 
wood, fiber-cement, metal, masonry or brick, LVP and architectural concrete, with engineered wood used in 
protected locations such as soffits. Detail assemblies with true corners and returns so edges read finished. Where 
height rules permit exposed foundation, treat it as part of the façade and do not leave raw CMU on public edges.
Avoid rustic treatments, distressed veneers, and decorative brackets that imitate historic styles. At stoops and 
entries, introduce tactile elements such as wood soffits or formed metal rails where they are protected from direct 
weather, adding warmth without compromising durability.  Buildings within a neighborhood may use consistent 
themes, form and colors, while avoiding complete repetitive copies of buildings.
5.2.2 Glass
Provide daylight and views while maintaining neighborly privacy. Balance transparency with depth and rhythm so 
façades feel open without exposing interiors to direct overlook. Use bay depth, considered mullion spacing, and 
well-designed guards to control sightlines, and avoid large unshaded panes that face bedrooms across narrow 
rights-of-way. Select glazing that supports comfort and energy performance in a high-altitude climate without 
resorting to mirror-like finishes.
Admit light strategically with clerestories, corner windows set back into the massing, and pocket terraces or loggias 
that shade glass and add privacy. Calibrate these elements to each elevation so living spaces feel bright and 
connected while bedrooms and private rooms remain discreet.  
 
5.2.3 Building Colors
The color palette shown represents a family of tones but does not dictate how colors must be allocated or require 
the use of specific colors. Use quiet, maintainable palettes that reinforce neighborhood coherence. Favor families 
of related tones drawn from muted neutrals and mineral hues that pair well with masonry, timber, and metal. 
Coordinate balcony and guard finishes, as well as storefront and lobby framing, with adjacent cladding so the 
composition reads as one. Avoid high-chroma colors and high-gloss finishes on principal elevations, and choose 
coatings proven for UV exposure and de-icing salts.
Allow measured variety across a block through subtle shifts in tone and texture to avoid monotony while keeping 
a cohesive district character. Reserve stronger contrasts for limited moments such as entries or key details rather 
than large fields.

6.0 LANDSCAPE DESIGN CRITERIA
6.1 PUBLIC OPEN SPACE DESIGN
6.1.1 Purpose and Intent
The purpose of this document is to provide insight and guidance in the creation of a clear and cohesive landscape 
identity that aligns and supports both the “Mountain Modern” architectural approach and the local development 
guidelines. It offers integrated and synergistic design solutions while fostering a distinctive and unique 
placemeking aesthetic. 
6.1.2 Placemaking Approach
The primary focus of placemaking in this area involves integration and connectivity with the existing land uses 
while seamlessly introducing new elements tailored to the use of residential, retail, transit, and open space.
The landscape design must take the local climate into account to create safe and comfortable spaces for 
pedestrian travel to promote an active lifestyle. 
  In order to achieve this, five distinct landscape typologies have been identified to complement the site and its
  proposed uses. 
• Bridge & Arrival Landscape (Zone A)
• Residential Landscape (Zone B)
• Commercial Landscape (Zone C)
• Emerald Necklace Landscape (Zone D)
• Preservation Landscape (Zone E)

DAKOTA PACIFIC REAL ESTATE
6.4 KEY INTERSECTIONS
6.4.2 Pedestrian-Oriented Intersection
  In pedestrian-oriented intersections, pedestrians are the priority, so it is essential vehicular traffic is designed
  around this idea. The design must effectively separate pedestrians and moving traffic through the use of wide 
  sidewalks, or other physical barriers such as bollards and landscape buffers. Crosswalks clearly mark and align with
  sidewalks, and pedestrian crosswalk signals are automatic or easy to use. The pedestrian crossing distance and
  exposure should be kept at a minimum to avoid any possible conflicts, and traffic should be slowed through various
  traffic calming tactics to allow drivers maximum reaction time. The intersections reflect the overall circulation context
  of the site by creating a safe and inviting atmosphere. Pedestrian visibility should be maximized through lighting,
  surfacing texture and materials variation, exposed views, and a welcoming environment.
  Given the user group, which includes seniors and children, tabletop crossings are highly suggested at the intersection
  of Tech Center Dr. and Landmark Dr. The tabletop crossing enables users to cross roads at the sidewalk level,
  enhancing safety by slowing down oncoming vehicles. Furnishings, trees, and poles sit outside the clear sight
  triangles. 
6.4.3 Vehicular Intersection
  To accommodate necessary bus movements and potential traffic, the number of pedestrian crossing points must be
  minimized on the south of Landmark Dr. to optimize vehicle flow and reduce congestion. Clear signage and high
  visibility crosswalks can further enhance both pedestrian and driver safety while improving traffic flow. Coordinate
  plow routes and meltwater paths so winter operations do not degrade crossings.

canopies and ornamental grasses 
establish a naturalistic and sheltered 
environment.

ARRIVAL & BRIDGE LANDSCAPE
This landscape includes native species 
adapted to local conditions, as well as 
more striking ornamental shrubs and 
trees suited for the climate that require 
minimal maintenance and water.

RESIDENTIAL LANDSCAPE
This landscape aims to create a 
welcoming and multi-functional space. 
Plants in this landscape typology serve 
to both enhance aesthetics and provide 
natural protection for users in key areas.
This zone provides a year round visually 
appealing naturalistic design that 
aesthetically connects all open space 
areas on the site.

PRESERVATION INTERFACE
This zone implements strategies 
building around the existing native 
landscape bordering the project’s 
western and southern area. Interference 
is minimized, with planting mimicking 
the existing palette to create a landscape 
buffer while providing connectivity to 
the existing trail system.

6.0 LANDSCAPE DESIGN CRITERIA
6.7	 LANDSCAPE	DESIGN	PRINCIPLES	—	PLANTING	
 
The planting selection should be native and regionally adapted species tolerant of freeze–thaw, snow load, and 
de-icing salts that aim to replicate the natural structure found in the adjacent surroundings. The planting design 
should draw inspiration from the natural distribution pattern found in the local landscape, while simultaneously 
aiming to evoke a sense of unquiteness, authenticity, and hierarchy.  
  The Arrival & Bridge Landscape adopts a more formal and manicured planting pattern functioning as an
  impactful gateway into the site. The Residential Landscape offers a soft, irregular planting layout that enhances the
  visual appeal. The Commercial Landscape focusing mainly in areas with a lot of parking. It integrates canopy trees,
  grasses, and bioswales to shade and filter parking lot runoff. The Preservation Interface Landscape will feature
  dense planting creating a landscape buffer while simultaneously providing a blended edge condition to seamlessly
  integrate with the native landscape and connecting trails. The Emerald Necklace Landscape provides a visually
  appealing curvilinear design that connects all open space ares in the project.
6.8	 LANDSCAPE	DESIGN	PRINCIPLES	—	IRRIGATION
6.8.1	 Water 	Efficiency	and	Conservation
The goal of the master plan is to design a community that utilizes minimal irrigation through the use appropriate 
water-wise planting. Any irrigation needed will be handled through efficient irrigation systems. A large percentage 
of the landscape areas should be designed to minimize water run-off and water loss. Any retained runoff collected 
must be utilized and recycled into the irrigation system. Compliance and alignment with the county’s waterwise 
landscaping code is also to be ensured. 
6.8.2 Irrigation in Planting Area
Provide blow-out points and isolation valves for winterization; protect backflow assemblies from view within 
architectural or landscape elements; coordinate sleeves and depth with snow equipment and future maintenance. 
Ensure emitter placement and mulch profiles avoid ice accretion on walks.

6.0 LANDSCAPE DESIGN CRITERIA
6.9 LANDSCAPE DESIGN PRINCIPLES
6.9.1 Parking Lot
Break surface fields into small courts with canopy trees at regular intervals at islands with minimum width of 5’.  
provide shaded pedestrian aisles from stalls to entries; integrate snow storage pockets that do not conflict with 
trees or lighting; treat stormwater with pretreatment and infiltration/detention appropriate to alpine soils and 
freeze–thaw cycles.  
6.9.2 Site Furnishing
Site furnishings should reflect the architectural design and remain consistent with the overall cohesive character of 
the surrounding buildings. Establishing site unity through furnishings, detailing, and organization will enhance the 
aesthetic appeal and user experience of the site.  In the above chapter, the plant typology has been introduced as 
a feature that programs the space; the Preservation Interface Landscape that surrounds the entire site; Residential 
Landscape as an aesthetic and functional shelter maker; Commercial Landscape as a naturalistic palette that 
enhances site sustainability; Arrival & Bridge Landscape that welcomes visitors to the site and provides the initial 
aesthetic impact; Emeral Necklace landscape that connects the opens space areas. Site materials and furnishings 
will align with landscape typologies and the site program it creates.  
6.9.3 Plant Palette
Develop a signature palette for each typology drawing primarily from native and climate-adapted species. Specify 
species, cultivars, and sizes; define minimum soil volumes (tree pits and continuous trenches), staking/removal 
timing, and establishment watering protocols. Provide a one-page Signature Plant Palette plate summarizing 
canopy/understory/groundcover with seasonal notes and maintenance cues

7.0 SIGNAGE AND LIGHTING CRITERIA
7.1 SIGNAGE GUIDELINES  
7.1.1 General Signage
Signs should reinforce a clear, contemporary mountain identity and aid wayfinding without visual clutter. Use 
durable, repairable materials such as metal, glass, and wood in protected locations, with crisp fabrication that 
conceals fasteners and power. Keep copy concise and prioritize legibility for pedestrians first and drivers second. 
Integrate placement with architectural features such as bays, mullions, and transoms so signs strengthen the 
façade’s order and are proportioned to the spaces they serve.
Favor halo-lit or externally lit dimensional letters and edge-lit panels where appropriate, matching color 
temperature to adjacent lighting so the street reads consistent and comfortable at night.  Signage must comply 
with existing Sign Code and lighting ordinances except where this document outlines alternative standards and 
guidance.
7.1.2 Ground Level Signs
Freestanding and monument signs mark district entries, the plaza, and structured parking access, and they should 
be scaled to their setting while protecting sight triangles and pedestrian clear zones. Set each sign in a landscaped 
pad or on a hardscape plinth that is integrated with the streetscape, and maintain clear visibility at intersections 
and driveways. Provide unobtrusive lighting using shielded, full-cutoff fixtures, and keep drivers, transformers, and 
maintenance access out of view. Coordinate finishes and colors with the district’s furnishing palette so ground 
signs feel native to the public realm rather than applied.  
7.1.3 Building Mounted Signs
Wall signs, blade signs, canopy soffit signs, and window graphics should identify entries and tenants while keeping 
façades legible. Mount signs within clear architectural zones such as sign bands, between mullions, or on canopy 
fascias, and avoid covering joints, reveals, or elements that express the structure. Size blade signs for pedestrian 
viewing distances and maintain the required vertical and horizontal clearances over walks and doors. Keep window 
graphics minimal and avoid large opaque fields that eliminate transparency on active frontages. Coordinate tenant 
sign colors and finishes with the building’s color strategy and limit the palette so the composition reads ordered 
and cohesive. 
DRAFT - V4
40
39
[KIMBALL JUNCTION WEST] Architectural Design Standards11-18-2025
 AO Architects
DAKOTA PACIFIC REAL ESTATE
7.1 SIGNAGE GUIDELINES 
7.1.4 Temporary Signs
Use short-term signs for leasing, events, and construction in a way that protects district identity. Keep their 
duration and size limited, place them where they do not conflict with accessibility, sight lines, or operations, and 
remove them promptly when the permit expires. Prefer printed fabric or rigid panels with tidy edge details, and 
avoid ad-hoc banners and cable tie installations. 
7.1.5 Prohibited Signs
To protect night sky, pedestrian comfort, and architectural clarity, prohibit roof-mounted signs; animated, flashing, 
or video displays; exposed raceways and conduit; cabinet boxes with translucent faces on primary façades; and 
outlining buildings with linear LEDs.  
7.1.6 Submittal / Approval Procedure
Provide a sign package for each project phase: location plan; elevations/sections with mounting details; materials/
finishes; lighting method and photometric intent; and a maintenance plan. Coordinate with overall building and 
landscape submissions so placement, lighting, and power are resolved ahead of fabrication.

7.2 LIGHTING DESIGN GUIDELINES
7.2.1 General Lighting
Lighting should make walking intuitive and safe in all seasons while protecting dark skies and residential comfort. 
Use full-cutoff luminaires with no intentional uplight, and shield sources to prevent glare and spill into dwellings 
and the night sky. Keep color temperature warm and consistent at the pedestrian scale and coordinate it with 
adjacent signage lighting so the street reads as one place.
Provide simple controls such as timeclocks or photosensors and reduce output after hours except for safety and 
egress. Favor bollards and low poles in intimate pedestrian areas, reserving taller poles for streets and larger courts 
where the wider scale requires them. 
The purpose of this section is to:
1.  Balance the environmental and sustainability goals set forth in the Snyderville Basin General Plan with the  
 need to provide safe lighting practices;
2.  To minimize light pollution for the enjoyment of the Snyderville Basin’s residents and visitors;
3.  To prevent the degradation of the nighttime visual environment by production of unsightly and dangerous  
 glare;
4.  To create lighting practices that promote the health and safety of the Snyderville Basin’s residents and   
 visitors;
5.  To prevent unnecessary waste of energy and resources in the production of excessive light or wasted light;
6.  To prevent interference in the use or enjoyment of property which is not intended to be illuminated at night  
 and the loss of the scenic view of the night sky due to increased urban sky-glow and light trespass.
 
7.2.2 Service Area
Keep lighting at loading, refuse, and utility zones utilitarian and contained. Mount shielded luminaires that keep 
light on the task and out of adjacent walks and windows, and avoid wall packs that create glare along public edges. 
Connect fixtures to controls that dim or switch off when areas are inactive, and integrate them with screening so 
equipment is not prominent from public views. Coordinate photometrics with gate swings, pavement markings, 
and camera views so light levels are adequate without overlighting.  
7.2.3 Building Lighting
Use architectural lighting to support entries, canopies, and key wayfinding moments without washing entire 
façades. Light doors, stairs, ramps, and address points, and avoid broad flood lighting of walls or rooflines. Recess 
or shield fixtures at canopies and soffits to eliminate glare toward sidewalks and dwellings, and detail mounts and 
conduit so snow and ice do not shed onto the public way. Where accents are appropriate, use narrow, targeted 
beams to reveal elements such as pilasters or reveals sparingly, and plan for safe replacement access without lifts 
in fragile landscape areas.  
7.0 SIGNAGE AND LIGHTING CRITERIA

[KIMBALL JUNCTION WEST] Architectural Design Standards11-18-2025

DAKOTA PACIFIC REAL ESTATE
7.2 LIGHTING DESIGN GUIDELINES
7.2.4 Parking Lighting
Provide safe, even illumination for both walking and driving while minimizing skyglow. Use full-cutoff luminaires 
and locate poles so light stays out of upper-story windows and the plaza, coordinating positions with tree canopies 
and snow-storage areas. In garages, use continuous, efficient fixtures with cutoff optics and controls zoned by 
occupancy or time, and avoid bright portal transitions that create glare on sidewalks. Scale pole heights to the 
context so faces remain visible and comfortable, and integrate emergency pathway lighting with the primary 
system rather than adding redundant fixtures.  Position parking lighting adjacent to parking areas. Minimize 
setbacks that would necessitate increased lumen output or additional fixtures to ensure functional lighting levels.
7.2.5 Pedestrian Lighting
Provide a continuous and comfortable layer of lighting for pedestrian areas, minimizing glare and skyglow, 
except along street edges where street lighting is present. Use shielded path lights, bollards, and low poles in the 
furnishing or planting zone, with step lights and handrail lighting at ramps and stairs. Coordinate color temperature 
with nearby site and building lighting, keep fixtures out of snow storage and plow paths, conceal conduit and 
drivers, and use simple controls that dim after hours while maintaining clear, accessible routes. 
7.2.6 Amphitheater Park Lighting
Lighting for Amphitheater Park shall be reviewed and approved separately, and event-related lighting is excluded 
from these standards.

8.0 SUSTAINABILITY
8.1 SUSTAINABLE ARCHITECTURE
8.1.1 General Sustainability
Walls, roofs, and glazing should control heat, air, and moisture with continuous air barriers, robust insulation at 
transitions, and rainscreen strategies may also be used where effective. Select glazing that manages solar gain and 
heat loss for Park City’s altitude and sun without mirror-like finishes. Detail assemblies for freeze–thaw, snow load, 
and de-icing salts so durability and ease of maintenance are treated as core performance requirements. Refer to 
the Development Agreement dated 7-28-25..
8.2 SUSTAINABLE ENERGY USE
8.2.1	 Efficient 	Heating,	Ventilation	&	Cooling	Systems
Prioritize efficiency to reduce operational carbon while maintaining comfort in a cold-climate, high-sun 
environment. Select and size systems to reflect the reduced loads achieved by the envelope, and use heat or 
energy recovery with simple, intelligent controls as the baseline approach. For residential buildings, prepare a 
Carbon and Electrification Plan that targets carbon neutrality through all-electric systems or verified offsets, and 
include a measurement and verification plan with predicted usage, metering, and performance reporting.
Favor high-efficiency heat pumps suited to cold weather, and confirm that defrost cycles and condensate 
management do not affect sidewalks or public edges. Provide demand-controlled ventilation with heat or energy 
recovery, and zone controls by exposure and use so spaces are comfortable without waste. Coordinate intake and 
exhaust locations with the façade modules to avoid staining, glare, and noise at the street. 
8.2.2 Renewable Energy Generation
Participate in the utility’s 100 percent renewable energy program when available for residential buildings.  At build-
out, include EV infrastructure with at least 5 percent of stalls equipped with charging stations and an additional 10 
percent EV-capable with conduit, panel capacity, and space for future chargers.
Group PV on the least visible roof zones and coordinate with rooftop screening so modules and racking are not 
seen from the public realm. Consider hybrid PV plus snow-melt only when a life-cycle analysis supports both 
energy and safety goals.

[KIMBALL JUNCTION WEST] Architectural Design Standards11-18-2025

DAKOTA PACIFIC REAL ESTATE
8.0 SUSTAINABILITY
8.3 SUSTAINABLE BUILDING MATERIALS
8.3.1 General Sustainable Building
Preference is given to assemblies and products with long service life, low maintenance, and documented 
environmental performance. Detailing for disassembly and repair is encouraged to extend life cycles and reduce 
waste  
8.3.2 Materials
Select primary cladding and structure for durability under UV, freeze–thaw, and de-icing salts; avoid coatings and 
finishes that require frequent replacement. Use regionally appropriate stone such as stone locally available in 
Browns Canyon quarries as a base or accent; employ engineered wood only in protected locations; and finish any 
permitted exposed foundation to match façade quality.  
8.3.3 Materials Sustainability Standards
Select and document materials that lower embodied carbon and toxic exposure, perform reliably in a high-alpine 
climate, and allow repair, reuse, and recovery at end of life. Prioritize proven durability, third-party transparency, 
and straightforward installation so sustainability is achieved in both specification and construction.
Document environmental attributes for major envelope and interior finish packages, including items such as 
environmental product declarations, recycled content, or certified wood where applicable. Use low-emitting 
adhesives, sealants, paints, and flooring to protect indoor air quality, and include a construction waste plan that 
scales to project size and identifies salvage and reuse opportunities. Favor modular, repairable components—
panelized cladding, rainscreen hardware, storefront systems—to simplify future maintenance, and choose de-icing-
resistant paving with published cold-climate performance data.
8.3.4 Building Placement
Use placement and orientation to lower energy demand and improve outdoor comfort. Where feasible as may 
be dictated by topography, circulation roads, lot shape, or other constraints, align massing on an east–west axis 
to manage solar gain, and use building-to-building relationships to create sheltered pedestrian corridors. Shape 
courtyards to capture winter sun and provide summer shade, and position entries close to grade so routes remain 
simple and accessible in all seasons.
Coordinate building locations with bridge approaches, transit access, and the microclimate needs of the central 
plaza so sustainability and mobility goals reinforce one another. Ensure service and parking do not interrupt 
primary pedestrian paths, and reserve roof and façade zones that support future renewable Energy and shading 
strategies without altering the skyline.

9.0 ARCHITECTURAL DESIGN REVIEW / SUBMITTALS
9.1 DESIGN REVIEW & SUBMITTAL PROCEDURES
Projects are reviewed for conformance with these Standards and the controlling approvals before construction 
begins. Review proceeds in an orderly sequence so site, building, landscape, signage, lighting, and sustainability 
decisions are coordinated and enforceable in the field.  Refer to the Development Agreement dated 7-28-25..  
9.2 DESIGN STANDARDS OVERVIEW
These Standards are the basis for design, submittal, and inspection. They establish a common language for 
massing and height, setbacks and step-backs, transparency and entries, materials and color, roof and equipment 
screening, public-realm design, signage, lighting, and sustainability measures. During review, applicants are 
expected to demonstrate conformance section-by-section and to document any requested alternatives with 
evidence that outcomes meet or exceed the stated intent.  
9.3 REVIEW / APPROVAL PROCEDURE
9.3.1 General Approval Procedures
Architectural Design Review Approvals. Prepare ADRA for each project area as applicable. Staff reviews for 
consistency with the approvals; the Planning Commission issues a recommendation, and the County Manager acts 
(approve/approve with modifications/deny). Approved ADRA are recorded and become binding. 
Final Site Plan (FSP). After Sketch Plan/Staff review, submit the FSP. Staff prepares a recommendation; the 
Planning Commission recommends; the County Manager issues a decision. Once all signatures are obtained, 
the FSP must be recorded within one (1) year of approval. Appeals of the Manager’s decision go to the County 
Council within the window provided, then to District Court on the administrative record. Refer to the Development 
Agreement dated 7-28-25.. 
Subdivision & Condominium Plats. Staff review → Planning Commission recommendation → County Manager 
decision → record within one (1) year. Appeals follow the same path as FSP decisions. 
Low-Impact Permits (if applicable). A streamlined path may be used where eligibility is met; the Community 
Development Director may require a public hearing and Planning Commission recommendation for community-
wide issues.

9.0 ARCHITECTURAL DESIGN REVIEW / SUBMITTALS
9.3 REVIEW / APPROVAL PROCEDURE
9.3.2 Submittal Requirements
Provide a complete, coordinated package scaled to the project scope. Refer to the Development Agreement dated 
7-28-25. and CC&Rs.  At minimum:
•  Context & site. Block/street context, setbacks and step-backs, grading and sections, utilities and stormwater,   
 access/service, snow management. (Where public infrastructure required by approval is not yet built, execute a  
 Development Improvements Agreement (DIA) consistent with the approvals.) 
• Architecture. Plans, elevations, and sections with height references; façade system sheets; roof plans and details  
 showing full equipment concealment within allowed height exceptions. 
• Public realm. Plaza/open-space plans; streetscape/lighting/planting; key intersection diagrams; trail connections  
 to the underpass/bridge and regional system (with phasing). 
•  Signage & lighting. Sign family locations, sizes, mounts, and lighting method; site and building lighting with   
 photometric intent consistent with dark-sky goals.
• Sustainability. Envelope strategy; Carbon & Electrification Plan (residential) with M&V; EV infrastructure counts  
 (≥5% EVSE installed + 10% EV-capable at build-out) and PV readiness.
• Matrices. Use/density/parking matrix (show residential minimums, commercial maximums, and any shared-  
 parking/TDM reductions requested).
9.3.3 Fees
Application and review fees are assessed per the applicable schedules at each stage (ADS, FSP, plats, permits). The 
applicant is responsible for any third-party reviews reasonably required to confirm compliance (e.g., peer traffic, 
shared-parking, sustainability/M&V). 
9.3.4 Variances
Where a numeric standard would limit performance or public-realm quality, an alternative may be requested. 
The submittal shall demonstrate equal or better outcomes relative to the stated intent and include supporting 
diagrams, metrics, and—where relevant—shared-parking/TDM studies. Approval follows the Manager path 
informed by Staff and Planning Commission recommendations, consistent with the controlling approvals.

9.0 ARCHITECTURAL DESIGN REVIEW / SUBMITTALS
9.4 LAWS, CODES & ORDINANCES
9.4.1 Subsequent Changes
These Guidelines may be amended. Approved amendments are recorded and supersede prior versions for new 
applications unless otherwise stated; previously approved projects continue under their approvals unless a 
modification is sought. 
9.4.2 Use of Design Consultants
The review body may require qualified consultants (e.g., architecture, landscape, lighting, traffic, sustainability) to 
assist in evaluating complex proposals or alternatives. Reasonable costs attributable to project-specific reviews are 
borne by the applicant when necessary to confirm compliance. 
9.4.3	 Alterations	or 	Modifications
Exterior alterations, additions, signage changes, lighting modifications, or site work after initial approval must be 
submitted for review proportional to the scope of change. Field changes that affect public-realm quality, massing, 
height, transparency, materials, or equipment screening require prior written approval.
9.4.4 Commencement of Construction
Construction may proceed after all approvals are granted, conditions satisfied, and applicable permits issued. 
Building permits may be phased (e.g., grading/foundation) provided each phase conforms to the approvals and 
these Guidelines.
9.4.5 Completion of Construction
Closeout includes submission of as-built drawings confirming conformance; certification that signage, lighting, 
and screening were installed as approved; and documentation of sustainability installations (EVSE counts, PV 
readiness, envelope details consistent with permit).  
9.4.6 General Rules for Contractors and Service Personnel
Contractors shall protect finished public-realm elements and adjacent properties; maintain safe, accessible 
pedestrian routes; manage dust, noise, and debris; and coordinate deliveries and staging to avoid blocking 
sidewalks, bus stops, or trail connections. Temporary wayfinding and lighting shall be provided where work affects 
public routes; damaged improvements shall be repaired or replaced in kind before closeout.

Proposal 
The applicant, Colin Hilton, is requesting several amendments to the Utah Olympic Park Specially 
Planned Area Development Agreement (DA). The applicant’s written description of the proposal 
is attached as Exhibit 1 and the redlined Development Agreement is attached as Exhibit 2. *Note 
that this draft may not include all changes made between the versions published on August 26, 
2025 and October 24, 2025. The Applicant is working to combine the two documents and ensure 
that all changes are reflected in the final proposed amendments prior to Council hearing. 
 
There are five primary changes proposed: 
1. Replacing the Final Site Plan with a Master Conceptual Development Plan (MCDP) that 
shows all existing buildings in their as -built locations and planned areas of future 
development. This includes rearranging the parcels shown on the original Final Site Plan. 
*Note that there have been some modifications to the MCDP since the October Planning 
Commission Meeting. 
2. Modifying the Land Use & Zoning Chart 1 to adjust the maximum building height, 
maximum gross floor area, and principal uses for several parcels.  *Changes to the MCDP 
are reflected in the draft chart included – to be updated at the Commission Meeting. 
3. Modifying the Land Use & Zoning Chart 2 to move three uses (Hotel, Inn; Ski Lift, New; 
and Ski Run, New) from Conditional to Low Impact. 
49
 
4. Modifying the way that Affordable Athlete and Workforce Housing Units are calculated. 
5. Requesting the County Council designate the UOP as a “Major Sporting Event Venue,” 
pursuant to SB333, 2025 General Legislative Session. 
 
Vicinity Map 
 
 
Background 
Most of the existing development on the site was done prior to the 2002 Olympic Games and 
largely approved through the State. The Utah Olympic Park (UOP) Specially Planned Area (SPA) 
designation and rezone was approved by the Council in 2013 (Ordinance 820).  
 
On August 26, 2025, the Snyderville Basin Planning Commission (Commission) held a work 
session to discuss the proposed amendments. The main discussion points included:  
• Traffic & transit 
• Affordable unit equivalents (AUEs) 
• Major Sporting Event Venue 
Designation 
• Current build out 
• Timeline & phasing 
• Details of the hotel plans 
• Proposed Location of Parcel 6 
• Change of Use Review 
• Public Benefits 
 
On October 14, 2025, the Snyderville Basin Planning Commission (SBPC) reviewed the proposed 
amendments with the applicant, opened a public hearing, and ultimately requested that the 
item be continued to November 25th, 2025 in order to allow the applicant time to provide 
additional information as requested by the Public and the Commissioners. A summary of the 
discussion items can be found below.

Process for Amending the DA  
The DA outlines a process for a substantial amendment and a process for an administrative 
amendment as shown below. Due to the various proposed changes, Staff finds that the 
proposed amendment falls under the substantial amendment process.  
    
  Image B: Process for Amending the DA 
 
   
 
Applicable Development Code Requirements 
The original approval of the Utah Olympic Park was done through a Specially Planned Area 
(SPA) process which also requires a Master Planned Development. According to Development 
Code 10-3-11, a Major SPA Plan Modification shall be reviewed by the Snyderville Basin 
Planning Commission who will make a recommendation to County Council. 
Major SPA Plan Modification Standards (10-3-11)(6)) 
Standard 1: Circumstances relevant to the request have changed or were unknown at the time 
of the original SPA plan approval.  
 
COMPLIES: At the time of the original SPA approval, the Final Site Plan in Exhibit C was 
created based on anticipated development plans. Since that time, the location of several 
buildings have been moved. The proposed Master Conceptual Development Plan 
(MCDP) would accurately reflect as-built locations for existing buildings and update the 
anticipated building locations for future development. 
 
Standard 2: The modification will not otherwise alter any of the findings required for the 
approval of the original SPA. 
 
DISCUSSION: Staff finds that with the proposed modifications, the Development 
Agreement is still in line with the Snyderville Basin General Plan. However, Staff finds 
55
 
that the proposed modifications result in an increase in floor area and will therefore 
require additional impact mitigation (such as traffic mitigation and affordable housing). 
Staff proposes a Condition of Approval to require these revisions be made prior to 
Council’s final decision. 
 
Standard 3: The modification is generally consistent with the efficient development and 
preservation of the entire SPA Plan.  
 
COMPLIES: The amendments are consistent with the original SPA plan and guiding 
principles. The modifications will clarify and refine the document.   
 
Standard 4: The modification does not affect in a substantially adverse manner either the 
enjoyment of land abutting upon or in the general vicinity of the property in question.  
 
DISCUSSION: The Applicant has hosted two open houses since our last Public Hearing 
with the goal of educating the public on the details of the project and working with 
residents to modify the plans to address their core concerns. For example, several 
concerns were raised relating to Parcel 6. To address these concerns, the Applicant has 
removed this requested new parcel from the proposed amendments and has instead 
gone back to the original parcel locations of parcels 13 & 14 (though they are now 
labeled 6A and 6B). This will significantly reduce any adverse impacts on surrounding 
neighbors. The Applicant is also working on addressing the other concerns raised by 
neighbors, to the best of their abilities, prior to a Council hearing. 
 
Standard 5: The public health, safety and general welfare are not adversely impaired by the 
modification.  
 
COMPLIES, as conditioned: Staff finds that if the Affordable Housing and Traffic Impact 
Study are revised to reflect the proposed Maximum Floor Area and the Applicant 
provides the necessary items to alleviate those impacts, that public health, safety and 
general welfare will not be adversely affected by the proposed amendments. 
 
Master Planned Development Standards (10-2-18(D)(6)) 
Additionally, Development Code 10-2-18(D)(6) states that a major amendment to a Master 
Planned Development (MPD) is defined as an amendment that increases square footage, 
density, or intensity of the previously approved MPD.

Utah Olympic Park Specially Planned Area Development Agreement Amendment 
Written Description of Proposal  
 
Approved in 2013 and extended in 2023, the Specially Planned Area for the Utah Olympic Park (“Park”) 
included a Master Plan intended to be updated over time. With the recent award of the 2034 Winter 
Games and an updated 10-year Utah Olympic Legacy Foundation (UOLF) Strategic Plan with Utah 
Olympic Park (UOP) updated Conceptual Master Plan, UOLF has submitted an application to amend the 
Development Agreement to reflect the latest strategic initiatives of UOLF, and more specifically the 
Utah Olympic Park.  
 
The UOLF sees the execution of the updated Master Plan at the UOP as a very critical component to 
ensure that the UOP can continue to provide world-class training and educational opportunities to 
athletes and the greater community. Broadly speaking, UOLF has the following long-term venue goals: 
• Create Financially Sustainable Sport & Community Uses of Olympic & Paralympic Venues 
• Inspire Utahns to be Active in Sport & Physical Activity 
• Maintain World Class Venues / Facilities 
• Engage the community and visitors alike in unique facility offerings 
 
UOLF also has the following specific UOP Financial Objectives that were reviewed with the County in 
2013. They were important back then, and just as important today given our very challenging 
subsidized program and venue cost dynamic. 
 
Increase Legacy Foundation revenues and control expenses by… 
1 |  Growing Public Program Revenues 
2 |  Advancing Fundraising & Strategic Partner Financial Support 
3 |  Promoting effective Land Planning & New Building Uses at Utah Olympic Park 
And always look to …. 
4 |  Reducing costs through creating efficiencies & reprioritizing budget needs 
 
Relating to point #3 above, UOLF created the following “Development Purpose” for any new 
construction under the UOP L-T Master Plan: 
• Promote More Activity - Proactively increase Public & Sport Uses of the Olympic Park. Further 
enhance and add to our unique offerings. 
• Create an added revenue streams through smart development - Lowering the dependence on 
the UOLF Legacy Fund earnings to cover UOP operating subsidies. UOP’s current net operating 
loss is approximately ($2.5m)/year. 
• Ensure the Olympic Park stays in existence in perpetuity, under an independent 501C3 
Foundation, not run by State or Local Gov’t . 
• Create a Dynamic Sports & Recreation Campus  
• Build additional facilities & services that support & complement Olympic & Paralympic Winter 
Sport Facilities & Programs

Article 2 
RECITALS 
2.1 The recitals in the remainder of this Article 2, together with the findings set forth 
in Article 3, are an integral part of this Development Agreement and are a part of 
the consideration for each Party's entry into this Development Agreement. 
2.2 Developer is the record owner of the Project or has contractual rights to acquire 
any such portions thereof as are not owned of record by Developer. Developer, 
County and the Snyderville Basin Special Recreation District have entered into 
that certain Exchange Agreement, dated November 6,2013, attached hereto as 
Exhibit J, whereby various parcels of property have been exchanged between 
the entities. Such exchanges have enabled Developer to relocate portions of the 
proposed residential uses at the Project in order to, among other benefits, further 
minimize visibility to and from Kimball Junction. It is further agreed that such 
exchange will not alter the overall density rights for the Project. 
2.3 This Development Agreement serves to implement the Olympic Park SPA and 
Olympic Park SPA Plan Book of Exhibits which are incorporated by reference 
herein. 
2.4 This Development Agreement and the Olympic Park SPA Plan resolve all issues 
associated with the development and construction of the Project except, as to the 
performance of any additional obligations which shall be specified in this 
Development Agreement. 
2.5 The County has adopted through Ordinance No. 819 an amendment to the 
General Plan, the Code and the Zoning Map classifying the Project as a Resort 
Center (RC) zone and setting forth therein such land use classifications and 
development locations as are permitted under thethis Development Agreement. 
65
WORKING DRAFT
2.6 Developer has proposed specific plans and plats with respect to the Project in 
response to direction and  guidance from the Director and the Planning 
Commission. 
2.7 The County therefore desires to establish the Olympic Park SPA under the SPA 
provisions of the Code and the General Plan for the purpose of implementing 
development standards and processes that are consistent therewith. 
2.8 The Development Agreement, which implements the Olympic Park SPA, provides 
detailed data regarding the Final Site Plans for the Project. The County and 
Developer agree that each shall comply with the standards and procedures 
contemplated by the Olympic Park SPA, this Development Agreement and its 
accompanying Exhibits, the Code, and the General Plan with respect to obtaining 
future Building Permits for any Phases or new construction within the Project. 
2.9 Developer and the County desire to clarify certain standards and procedures that 
will be applied to approvals of Building Permits for the Phases of the 
development and construction and to address requirements for certain Public 
Benefits. 
2.10 The County also desires to receive certain Public Benefits, and Developer is 
willing to provide these Public Benefits in consideration of the agreement of the 
County for increased intensity of uses in the Olympic Park SPA pursuant to the 
terms of this Development Agreement. 
2.11 The County, acting pursuant to its authority under Utah Code Annotated, Section 
17-27a-101 et seq., the Code and the General Plan, has made certain 
determinations with respect to the Olympic Park SPA, and, in the exercise of its 
legislative discretion, has elected to approve the uses, density, and general 
configuration of the Project and its future development pursuant to the Olympic 
Park SPA, resulting in  the negotiation, consideration and approval of this 
Development Agreement, after all necessary public hearings.

public of the Snyderville Basin that significantly outweigh those that would be 
derived if the development occurred under the provisions of the existing zone. 
The provisions of those benefits and amenities have been taken into 
consideration by Summit County in granting increased residential and 
commercial densities on the Project. 
3.5 The Olympic Park SPA Plan, as reflected in and conditioned by the terms and 
conditions of the Development Agreement and the amendments thereto, is in 
conformity and compliance with the General Plan, any existing capital 
improvement programs, the provisions of the Code (including concurrency and 
infrastructure requirements), and all other development requirements of the 
County. 
3.6 The Olympic Park SPA Plan contains outstanding features which advance the 
policies, goals and objectives of the General Plan beyond mere conformity, 
including the following: (i) agreements with respect to design controls and 
limitations to minimize the visual impact of the Project; (ii) dedication and 
preservation of view shed and environmental features; (iii) taking advantageous 
use of natural depressions in topography to minimize the visual prominence and 
potential for ridge lining; (iv) appropriate location of density and uses; (v) the 
preservation of critical open space areas; (vi) the promotion of recreation uses 
and resort related Public Benefits that are appropriate to support the community 
and recreational nature of the area; (vii) contribution to community trails and 
parks including working with the Snyderville Basin Special Recreation District to 
identify possible trail linkages and trail heads; (viii) exceeding open space 
requirements for the Project; and (ix) using efforts to minimize visibility of the 
residential housing to and from Kimball Junction; (x) provides incentives to 
private development partners to avoid depleting the endowment established 
to fund the operation of the venues operated by the Developer. 
3.7 There are unique circumstances that justify the use of a SPA, including 
preserving the long- term viability of the Olympic Park. County and Developer 
acknowledge that the existing facilities were built by the State of Utah without 
any zoning or building approvals. In order to preserve the long- term viability of 
67
WORKING DRAFT
the Olympic Park, additional funding is needed to avoid depleting the 
endowment established to fund the operation of the Olympic Park. Expanded 
commercial development within the Olympic Park SPA is one way to provide this 
funding. 
3.8 Developer has committed to comply with all Affordable Housing Requirements 
consistent with other resort areas in Summit County and as delineated in Exhibit 
H hereinof Chapter 5 of the Code. County acknowledges and agrees that to the 
extent that Developer exceeds the minimum unit equivalent Affordable Housing 
Requirements under Exhibit Hthe Code, Developer may use such additional 
housing in any manner it sees fit consistent with the terms of this Agreement 
including or the existing Code provisions for residential uses or Hotel, 
Inn/Lodging, including, but not limited to, charging a higher rental rate and 
utilizing such housing as nightly rentals for visiting athletes, Olympic Park 
guests, or officials. 
3.9 Subject to the terms of this Development Agreement, Developer has committed 
to comply with all appropriate concurrency and infrastructure requirements of the 
Code, and all appropriate criteria and standards described in the Development 
Agreement and the amendments thereto, including any applicable impact fees of 
the County and its Special Districts (subject to Articles 6.1 and 6.2 below). 
3.10 There exist adequate provisions for mitigation of all fiscal and service impacts on 
the general public. 
3.11 Any construction management impacts shall be addressed in the Construction 
Management and Mitigation Plan established for the Olympic Park SPA and 
approved by the County Engineer, as required in Article 4.6 hereof. 
3.12 The approval of the Olympic Park SPA Plan will not adversely affect the public 
health, safety and general welfare of the residents of Summit County, it being 
acknowledged that the proposed Project will substantially enhance and benefit 
the public health, safety and general welfare of the residents of Summit County. 
3.13 The Olympic Park SPA Plan meets or exceeds development quality and 
aesthetic objectives of the General Plan and the Code, is consistent with the 
goal of orderly growth in the Snyderville Basin, and minimizes construction 
impacts on public infrastructure within the Snyderville Basin. 
3.14 The proposed development reasonably assures life and property within the 
Snyderville Basin is protected from any adverse impact of its development, it 
being acknowledged that the proposed Project will substantially enhance and 
benefit life and property within the Snyderville Basin. 
3.15 Developer shall take reasonably appropriate measures to prevent harm to 
neighboring properties and lands from development, including nuisances. 
68
WORKING DRAFT
3.16 Exemption from Code. The County Council acting pursuant to its authority under 
Utah Code Annotated 17-27a-101 et seq., as well as its regulations and 
guidelines, in the exercise of its legislative discretion, has determined that the 
Olympic Park SPA is exempt from the application of the Code solely to the extent 
that such a finding may be a condition precedent to approval of this 
Development Agreement. Where there is a direct conflict between an express 
provision of this Development Agreement and the Code or General Plan or other 
Land Use Laws, this Development Agreement shall take precedence; otherwise, 
the Code, General Plan, or other Land Use Laws shall control.
