119
To the Summit County Planning Commission, We are writing on behalf of the SunPeak Homeowner’s Association (HOA) to formally submit our concerns regarding the proposed amendment to the Utah Olympic Park (UOP) developer agreement. We believe the UOP’s proposal and the associated staff report fail to address critical impacts on our neighborhood and the larger Snyderville Basin community. We have met with UOP leaders and have had productive conversations aimed at addressing the proposal's significant adverse effects on our community. We are continuing to work collaboratively with the UOP toward tangible, written solutions that can provide our neighborhood with the necessary protections while respecting the UOP's mission.  Where appropriate, we have included notes on unaddressed concerns as well as proposed next steps and potential solutions that have emerged from our conversations. While this dialogue is still ongoing, we find that the application as currently written and the associated staff reports are missing essential information for the Commission to make an informed decision and carry out its obligations. We ask that the Commission review and require the following:  1. Gate Access, Traffic Impacts, and Staff Report Conclusions This amendment introduces the potential for new, routine traffic through the gate connecting Olympic Parkway and Bear Hollow Drive. We believe the staff report's conclusions on this issue are inaccurate and incomplete.  The report and applicant proposal fail to adequately acknowledge, study, or address the impact this new use would have on Bear Hollow Drive or our neighborhood. • Disagreement with Staff Conclusion on Standard 4: We strongly disagree with the staff's conclusion that this amendment meets Standard 4 (no adverse effect on the vicinity). This conclusion fails to account for the new traffic that will be generated by both the proposed increase in development density and the UOP's expressed desire to begin using this gate for routine access. • Unanswered Questions on Road Safety & Integrity: Beyond the lack of traffic studies, there are fundamental, unanswered questions about the safety and structural integrity of Bear Hollow Drive itself. o A 2001 Sports Illustrated article investigating the road's construction noted that to avoid costly county and state standards, the job was assigned to the Utah Division of Facilities Construction & Management, an agency that "builds buildings, not highways."  The article describes the result as "a winding, two-lane road with grades exceeding county standards and prone to slides and sinkholes."  This is consistent with the recollection of community members who lived in SunPeak at the time the road was constructed.   o Our HOA has formally requested the county records related to Bear Hollow Drive, including engineering documents and safety records, to verify it is safe 
120
and built to code for its current use, let alone the proposed increase in traffic.  To date, the county has not responded with any documents to this request. • Inadequate Traffic Studies: The current traffic studies are insufficient. They fail to identify this new use case (routine gate access) and, therefore, do not analyze the specific impact this new volume of residential and commercial traffic will have on Bear Hollow Drive, a dead-end residential county road. • Disagreement with "No Change to Access" Conclusion: We disagree with the staff's conclusion that there are "no changes to access" under the master-planned development requirements of the code. A change from the gate's current non-use to a new, routine daily use is a fundamental and significant change to access for our community, one with the potential to have a substantial adverse effect. • Failure to Address Gate Use Expansion: The staff report and applicant ignore the established precedent for this gate. Any past use has been for emergency access only. This was a "permissive use" that the county and residents allowed for safety. This permissive allowance does not create a vested, legal right for the UOP to expand that use to general, routine traffic. Update from UOP Discussions The UOP has been consistent that its intent is to use the gate for routine traffic associated with some of the proposed residential development and has stated that it plans to provide our HOA with a draft, written gate use policy for our review prior to the Planning Commission meeting on November 25th. We see this as a positive first step that must be supported by the necessary legal and engineering documentation (as requested below) and ultimately be incorporated into the development agreement as a binding commitment. Our Request to the Commission: To fulfill its obligations, we request that the Planning Commission withhold a positive recommendation until the following items are provided and reviewed: 1. Require Formal, Legal Documentation: The UOP must be required to produce the formal, legal documentation (e.g., a recorded easement, right-of-way, or other agreement) that establishes its legal right to connect to and use Bear Hollow Drive for routine traffic. This documentation has been requested by our HOA and, to our knowledge, has not been produced, suggesting it may not exist.  If the UOP cannot prove this specific right for expanded use, the Commission must recognize the precedent created by historic use and the UOP must be required to formally apply for any new access right. 2. Require Road Safety & Engineering Documentation: The Commission must require the county and/or applicant to produce these documents or conduct the necessary studies and address any issues necessary to demonstrate that Bear Hollow is code-compliant and safe for the full impact of the proposed new traffic. 3. Define and Restrict Use: The applicant must provide clear, binding documentation defining the exact proposed uses of the gate, and any approved uses must be formalized as restrictions within the development agreement itself. 
121
4. Require an Updated Traffic Study: The applicant must be required to provide a new, independent traffic study that specifically analyzes the impact of the proposed uses on the safety, noise, and infrastructure of Bear Hollow Drive. Should the Planning Commission move forward with a positive recommendation without these essential items, we formally request a clear, transparent, and written explanation detailing why this information was not deemed necessary for the Commission to meet its legal and ethical obligations to our community.  2. Construction Traffic Mitigation The Snyderville Basin Development Code (Title 10) requires the applicant to mitigate the adverse impacts of construction (including traffic) on surrounding communities. The code's requirement exists for this exact purpose: to prevent harm and nuisance to neighboring properties. • Failure to Meet Obligation: Allowing the use of Bear Hollow Drive for any construction traffic would be a clear failure to meet this obligation, especially when an alternative exists. • Availability of Private Roads: The UOP has internal, private roads that are viable and appropriate routes for all its construction vehicles. Forcing this impact onto a residential county road when a private alternative exists fails to uphold the county's duty to protect our neighborhood. Our Request to the Commission: 1. We request that the Planning Commission require a permanent, binding restriction be placed directly into the developer agreement that prohibits all UOP construction traffic on Bear Hollow Drive. 2. Furthermore, we request this restriction be a required, non-negotiable component of all subsequently proposed Construction Mitigation Plans for any phase of this development. Should the Planning Commission move forward without this binding restriction, we formally request a clear and transparent explanation detailing why the Commission believes this is not necessary to meet its obligations, particularly when a clear, private alternative (the UOP's internal roads) is available.   3. Removal of Protected Open Space as a Public Benefit A core public benefit of the original developer agreement was the permanent preservation of open space. This amendment seeks to unilaterally remove that benefit without justification. 
122
• Breach of Original Public Benefit: The original agreement (Exhibit H) was secured based on the public benefit that 82% of the UOP would be designated open space. This open space was explicitly promised to be in "large, continuous areas" and "located to protect scenic hillsides, viewsheds, and ridgelines." • Violation of Agreement (Section 7.1.1): The applicant has an obligation to protect this open space under Section 7.1.1 of the existing agreement. • Misleading Revised Master Plan: The new proposal makes Section 7.1.1 misleading, as the new Master Conceptual Site Plan shows no designated open space. • Failure to Meet Standard 2: Open space protections are a key component of the requirements under the original Development Agreement. The UOP has provided no valid justification for removing this foundational public benefit and has proposed no new or additional public benefits to replace it. Updates from UOP Discussions The UOP has expressed a concerning legal interpretation regarding their right to build residential and commercial structures on areas designated “recreational open space” in the original development agreement. They have stated that, based on a footnote on the zoning chart in Exhibit C of the original agreement, they feel they have no binding obligation to protect designated open spaces, and have no restriction on development in these areas, including the proposed parcels 6, 7, and 8. This interpretation appears to be in direct conflict with specific provisions addressing the open space in the original Development Agreement and the fundamental intent of the Development Agreement. The UOP was afforded increased density and use allowances in exchange for public benefits, chief among them the preservation of this very open space. In addition, these designated open spaces create a natural buffer between UOP development, the sensitive ridgeline, and our neighborhood, creating an essential protection against the very adverse effects of noise, light, and incompatible use raised throughout this letter.  The staff report confirms this amendment requests a "significant increase in density," yet new public benefits to offset this request are absent. Worse, the UOP's interpretation would remove an essential and foundational public benefit that was the basis for the original agreement. Our Request to the Commission: 1. Formally and Permanently Prohibit Development on Designated Open Space: The Commission must affirm the permanent open space designation and remove all development rights from it. The protection of open space is a foundational public benefit that the Commission must ensure is protected in this current amendment and any future amendments proposed by the UOP.  Should the Planning Commission move forward with a positive recommendation without enforcing the original open space is preserved, we formally request a clear, transparent, and 
123
written explanation detailing what justification the Commission used to remove the UOP’s obligation to protect open space as a foundational public benefit.  4. Development on Parcel 6 (Designated Open Space) The proposal to add significant new residential density to Parcel 6 is a major change from the existing agreement.  This parcel is a critical buffer zone, and its development would immediately impact the adjacent SunPeak community.  The extensive impacts of this change must be thoroughly studied and addressed to ensure the SunPeak community is not adversely affected. • Disagreement with Staff Conclusion on Standard 4: We again disagree with the staff's conclusion under Standard 4. This conclusion completely fails to account for the new, large-scale residential development proposed for Parcel 6. The incongruity of this dense development with the surrounding neighborhood, along with its associated noise, light, traffic, and other impacts, constitutes a substantial adverse effect. • Failure to Meet MPD Requirements: The staff report incorrectly concludes that Master-Planned Development requirements are met. o Designing with Adjacent Uses: We disagree with the staff's conclusions. The staff report fails to acknowledge the potential impact of traffic, light, and noise pollution on the surrounding neighborhood. It also ignores the fundamental incompatibility of the proposed development (potential height variances, dense housing vs. established single-family lots and homes) with the adjacent uses. o Building Location: We disagree with the staff's conclusions, as the proposal directly impacts critical lands previously designated as open space. • Violation of Existing Agreement: The current developer agreement, under Section 7.1.1, designates this area as open space. The UOP has a contractual obligation to protect this land, not develop it. • Failure to Justify Amendment: The UOP has failed to offer an acceptable justification for this change as required under Standard 2 and Standard 3.  Furthermore, the Planning Commission has its own independent obligation to protect designated open space. • Missing Environmental Analysis: The previous environmental studies for the UOP are invalid for this proposal, as they analyzed Parcel 6 as open space. No new studies have been submitted to evaluate the true impact of this new development. Update from UOP Discussions In our discussions, the UOP has proposed a potential solution: to relocate the density currently proposed for Parcel 6 back to Parcels 13 and 14, which were areas approved for development in their 2013 agreement.  The UOP has also clearly stated that they intend to return with a subsequent amendment to propose new development on Parcel 6.  This does not solve the problem; it only postpones it and all of the concerns detailed above regarding open space, environmental impact, and incompatibility with our neighborhood remain. 
124
Our Request to the Commission: Our primary and uncompromised position is that development on Parcel 6 should not be permitted. This land is designated as protected open space in the current agreement and we ask the Commission to uphold its foundational obligation to enforce this public benefit. To that end, we request the Commission: 1. Formally and Permanently Prohibit Development on Parcel 6: The Commission must affirm the permanent open space designation of this parcel and remove all development rights from it. This is the only acceptable solution to address the UOP's stated intent to return with a subsequent development proposal for this parcel in a subsequent amendment. 2. Acknowledge Contingency Restrictions: The restrictions below are only necessary if the Commission decides not to uphold its foundational obligation to prohibit development on designated open space. These are non-negotiable minimum requirements in that scenario, not an acceptable alternative to the parcel's protected open space status. 1. Require New Environmental Studies: Mandate updated, specific studies to evaluate the potential adverse effects of this new development on the area's sensitive lands, critical wildlife habitats, and migration corridors. 2. Impose Development Restrictions: Any potential approval must include binding restrictions that ensure development is consistent with the surrounding area (e.g., a 32-foot height limit for single-family homes and strict limits on total density/square footage). 3. Impose Use Restrictions: Formally document and record strict use restrictions that prohibit short-term rentals and other commercial operations, which are incompatible with the adjacent residential land use. Should the Planning Commission move forward without these critical studies and restrictions, we formally request a clear and transparent explanation detailing why this information was not deemed necessary to meet its obligations under the code and the existing developer agreement.  5. Ridgeline Development (Parcels 7 & 8) and Commercial Use The proposal to develop new structures on Parcels 7 and 8 along the sensitive ridgeline separating our communities, combined with a reduction in oversight for new commercial uses, represents a significant change from the UOP’s current development agreement.  The impacts of this change must be thoroughly studied and addressed to ensure the SunPeak and larger Snydervillle Basin community are not adversely affected. • Disagreement on Standard 4 (Adverse Effect): We disagree with the staff's conclusions. The development introduces new, significant sources of light and noise 
125
pollution and will have an adverse impact on the viewshed, all of which are inconsistent with the surrounding neighborhood. • Disagreement on Standard 5 (Viewshed): We disagree with the staff's conclusion, as the proposal has a clear potential to negatively impact the protected viewshed from SR-224. The Commission has an obligation to protect this, which the staff report ignores. • Failure to Meet MPD Requirements: The staff report incorrectly concludes that Master-Planned Development requirements are met. o Development with Topography: The report fails to acknowledge the direct, negative impact of this proposal on a sensitive ridgeline. o Designing with Adjacent Uses: The report fails to acknowledge the incompatibility of proposed commercial uses and the resulting light and noise pollution with the adjacent residential community. o Building Location: The proposal to build on these parcels directly impacts critical lands, including designated open space and protected ridgelines. • Violation of Existing Agreement (Section 7.1.1): As with Parcel 6, these areas are designated as open space that the UOP is obligated to preserve. The UOP has failed to offer an acceptable justification for this change as required under Standard 2 and Standard 3. • Violation of County Ridgeline Code: The county code specifically prohibits development within 100 vertical feet of a ridgeline, with narrow exceptions for ski lifts and runs. This proposal for new structures appears to be in direct violation of this code. • Removal of Essential Public Safeguards: The UOP's request to reclassify new ski runs as "Low Impact" is an attempt to circumvent a required public process. The staff report for the Phase 2 of the UOPs ski lift/run Conditional Use Permit (CUP) clearly states the applicant must re-apply for a new CUP for Phase 3. This means none of the essential evaluations (for noise, lighting, safety, and environmental impact) have been conducted for this new phase. Changing this to a "Low Impact" use would recklessly remove the UOP's obligation to mitigate these adverse effects, which the CUP process is designed to ensure. Update from UOP Discussions The UOP confirmed its intent to place these structures out of view from the SunPeak ridgeline.  However, as currently purposed they would be visible from other protected viewsheds, such as Route 224.  The UOP also shared its desire to utilize these ridgeline structures for commercial purposes. This includes hosting events, dinners, and other activities that would involve bringing non-athlete, general public attendees to these new structures for commercial activities.   Our Request to the Commission: Our primary and uncompromised position is that development on Parcel 7 and 8 should not be permitted. This land is designated as protected open space in the current agreement and we ask the Commission to uphold its foundational obligation to enforce this public benefit.  In addition, ridgeline development of this nature is prohibited under the Summit County code. 
126
To that end, we request the Commission: 1. Formally and Permanently Prohibit Development on Parcel 7 & 8 (and all designated open space): The Commission must affirm the permanent open space designation of these parcels and remove all development rights from it.  2. Acknowledge Contingency Restrictions: The restrictions below are only necessary if the Commission decides not to uphold its foundational obligation to prohibit development on designated open space.  These are non-negotiable minimum requirements in that scenario, not an acceptable alternative to this parcel’s protected open space status. 1. Require New Environmental and Wildlife Studies: Mandate updated studies to evaluate the adverse effects of this development on sensitive lands, critical wildlife habitats, and known migration corridors along the ridgeline. 2. Require Viewshed Studies: Mandate a formal viewshed analysis to evaluate the impact of new structures, lighting, and ski operations as seen from both the adjacent neighborhood and SR-224. 3. Impose Strict Use Restrictions: Formally document and record use restrictions that prohibit all commercial/public-facing use in this area, limiting any structures to essential, non-public "athlete support" functions only. 4. Enforce County Ridgeline Code: We request the Commission enforce the county code and prohibit the construction of any new structures within 100 vertical feet of the ridgeline. 3. Reject "Low Impact" Re-classification: The Commission must reject the re-classification and uphold the existing requirement for a full Conditional Use Permit for any new ski runs (Phase 3). Should the Planning Commission move forward with a positive recommendation without these critical studies and restrictions, we formally request a clear and transparent explanation detailing why this information was not deemed necessary to meet its multifaceted obligations under the Snyderville Basin Development Code.  5. Exemption of "Support Uses" from All Development Limits The UOP's request to exclude "Athlete Support Uses" and "Olympic Park Support Uses" from development density limits, affordable housing calculations, and traffic studies creates a loophole for unchecked development, and critically, asks the County to abandon these standard controls without offering any corresponding new public benefit to justify such a major concession. • Failure to Meet Standards 2 & 3: This change fails to meet Standard 2 and Standard 3. The UOP has provided no valid justification or "changed circumstance" that warrants such a sweeping, unilateral exemption from the county's most basic development requirements. 
127
• This Change Removes Public Benefit:  This exemption offers no new or expanded public benefit; rather, it actively takes away existing public benefits and critical safeguards. It allows for: o Potentially unlimited, unmitigated development that is not accounted for in any density cap. o The circumvention of affordable housing obligations, which should accurately mirror the true development density and its impact on community services. o Fundamentally flawed traffic studies and mitigation efforts that fail to account for the full impact of the proposed changes. Our Request to the Commission: 1. We request that the Planning Commission categorically reject this proposed exemption. 2. The Commission must require that all development square footage, regardless of its internal classification by the UOP, be included in all density caps, affordable housing calculations, and traffic impact studies to ensure the full and actual impact of the project is properly analyzed and mitigated. Should the Planning Commission move forward with this exemption, we formally request a clear, transparent, and written explanation detailing what public benefit it believes justifies removing these essential community protections.  Conclusion and Request for Public Process As outlined above, we feel the current application and staff report are critically deficient. Essential data regarding traffic, legal access, open space protections, environmental impacts, and viewsheds is missing, and the staff's conclusions repeatedly fail to address the clear adverse effects this proposal will have on our community. We have recently learned that the Planning Commission may consider public input on this proposal to be concluded. We must respectfully remind the Commission that the County is obligated to hold a "noticed public hearing" for this Substantial Amendment. This obligation cannot be meaningfully met until the applicant clarifies the project's true density and the necessary traffic, legal, and environmental information is provided for public review. Holding a hearing without this critical information prevents the public from being fully informed and renders any public comment meaningless. Therefore, we request that after all the information requested in this letter is provided (or the Commission provides its written explanations for why such information is not necessary), the public be given adequate time to review all new materials and a new, formal opportunity to provide public comment to the Planning Commission. 
128
We thank you for your time and for your service to the community. We trust that you will hold the applicant and this process to the standards required by the code. Sincerely, The Board of the SunPeak HOA

Dear Planning Staff and Snyderville Basin Planning Commission, 
I’m a Park City resident, business owner, and parent of a 14-year-old freestyle skier who trains at the Utah 
Olympic Park. I fully support the Park’s modernization. These upgrades are good for Park City as a whole —
they strengthen our world-class reputation and sustain the Olympic legacy that benefits residents, visitors, 
and local businesses. 
We also understand that construction brings unavoidable disruption. My request is simple: 
first and foremost, 
please approve this amendment , and pair it with a safety-first phasing plan  that minimizes summer 
closures  and, when possible, schedules higher-risk work in winter . That balance protects athletes and the 
public while keeping the project on track for everyone’s benefit. 
This isn’t about a few athletes seeking special treatment; it’s about responsible scheduling that preserves a 
community resource, supports local jobs and tourism, and keeps Park City’s Olympic heritage thriving.

Basin Planning Commission’s review of the Utah Olympic Park amendment.

STAFF REPORT 
 
To:   Snyderville Basin Planning Commission   
From:   Amir Caus, AICP, Senior Planner  
Date of Meeting: November 25, 2025 
Type of Item:  MPD-Rezone (NMU-1), MPD-Conditional Use Permit, MPD-Low Impact  
   Permit, and Development Agreement – Possible Action 
Process:   Legislative and Administrative Review 
File:   24-087 
   
 
Proposal 
 
The applicant is requesting a Master Planned Development (MPD) for the Junction Commons, a 
19-building mixed use redevelopment project located at 6699 N Landmark Dr (Parcel FSE-1), 
Kimball Junction, Summit County, UT.  The applicant is proposing to Rezone Parcel FSE-1 from 
the Town Center Zone to the Neighborhood Mixed-Use 1 (NMU-1) Zone. 
 
The project includes the redevelopment of the upper portion of the property into a residential 
project while the lower portion of the property would be developed with additional infill 
development.  
 
The upper portion of the property is currently developed with ~98,000 sq. ft. of retail space. 
The applicant is proposing to demolish ~82,000 sq. ft. of the existing retail and replace it with a 
10-building residential project which includes 433 units (391 apartments and 42 townhomes) 
and a ~7,200 sq. ft. amenity/clubhouse space within one of the three apartment buildings. One 
of the buildings is proposed to be mixed use and would contain ~7,300 sq. ft. commercial. The 
applicant is also proposing two parking structures in the upper portion of the property. 
 
The lower portion of the property is currently developed with ~230,000 sq. ft. of retail space. 
The applicant is proposing to infill the parking area with a 9-building mixed-use project with 
ground floor commercial (~54,000 sq. ft.) and upper floor residential (77 apartments).  
 
The applications/processes involved in this project include; 
 
• MPD-Rezone to Neighborhood Mixed-Use 1 (NMU-1) Zone 
• MPD-Conditional Use Permit 
• MPD-Low Impact Permit 
• Development Agreement 
• Potential Final Subdivision/Condo Plat (for any for-sale units) 
131
2 
 
Project Description 
 
Project Name: Junction Commons Master Planned Development (MPD) 
Applicant(s):   Elliott Workgroup 
Property Owner(s):   SRE Ontario LLC 
Location:   6699 N Landmark Dr, Kimball Junction, Summit County, UT  
Zone District:   Town Center (TC) 
Parcel Number and Size: FSE-1 (37.32 acres) 
Type of Process:   Legislative (Rezone and Development Agreement) -   
    Administrative (Conditional Use Permit, Low Impact Permit,  
    and Final Subdivision Plat) 
Final Land Use Authority: Summit County Council (Rezone and Development    
    Agreement) and Snyderville Basin Planning Commission   
    (Conditional Use Permit, Low Impact Permit, and Final   
    Subdivision/Condo Plat) 
 
Vicinity Map 
 
 
 
Background 
 
Junction Commons (formerly Outlets Park City, formerly Tanger Outlets, formerly the Factory 
Stores at Park City) was originally built in 1991 with 208,669 sq. ft. through a Class II Permit (a 
previous version of the development code). Based on the 1990 Class II Permit file, it does not 
132
3 
 
appear that there were community benefits, and the review was based on infrastructure and 
design criteria. 
 
In 1999 the Summit County Board of County Commissioners approved a 92,567 sq. ft. addition 
to the center through the Factory Stores Park City Specially Planned Area and Development  
Agreement provisions of the Snyderville Basin Development Code. Based on the 1998 Specially 
Planned Area and Development Agreement file, the community benefits included a partial 
Snyderville Basin Special Recreation District trail improvement (~650 feet), seating areas within 
the site, and preservation of existing parks and open space areas. 
 
In 2014, the Summit County Council approved a SPA designation for a 23,500 sq. ft. retail 
addition through the Tanger Outlets Specially Planned Area and Development Agreement. 
During the 2014 review, the Planning Commission and Summit County Council required public 
benefits as part of the Development Agreement process which resulted in a cumulative 
financial contribution to the Peace House project.  
 
According to the Summit County Assessor’s Office, the total square footage today is 322,804 sq. 
ft. The existing uses include soft-good retail (~70 tenants), management offices, and a small 
restaurant space.  
 
The 1999 Development Agreement has expired, while the 2014 agreement for the 23,500 sq. ft. 
retail addition is still in effect (2024). The 1990 Class II Permit for the original portion of the 
development does not have an underlying Development Agreement. 
 
In June of 2024, the Summit County Community Development Department received an 
application for a rezone to the NMU-1 Zone.  
 
The Planning Commission held a pre-application work session for this project on April 9, 2024. 
During the meeting the applicant went over the history of the property and introduced the 
project. During the meeting the Planning Commission provided consensus that the overall 
project vision was acceptable.  
 
The Planning Commission held pre-application work sessions on April 9, 2024 and August 27, 
2024 where they confirmed that the redevelopment and the overall massing were acceptable 
but that they wanted to confirm that the affordable housing requirements were being met.  
 
The Planning Commission also held a work session on February 11, 2025. Since the Planning 
Commission had three new members that did not have background on the project, Staff and 
the applicant requested to; 
 
• Introduce the project, changes from previous version, and the phasing plan 
• Go over the background for the previous pre-application work sessions 
• Go over the details for the affordable housing per the previous Planning Commission 
request 
• Get any comments and direction from the Planning Commission for future meetings 
and discussion 
133
4 
 
• Confirm the consensus on the overall project vision, massing, and affordable housing 
• Confirm the overall process schedule and review 
o Phase 1 would be to review the Rezone Portion 
o Phase 2 would be to return with the applicable Conditional Use and Low Impact 
Permits 
• Return at a future meeting to discuss traffic and parking for each phase of the project 
 
The Planning Commission held a work session on March 25, 2025 where they discussed the 
traffic, parking, transportation and connectivity. Individual planning commissioners expressed 
concern over still having too much parking versus not enough parking, however the overall 
numbers were explained by the applicant, how they work, and when the parking structure 
would be triggered.  
 
The Planning Commission held a public hearing on September 23, 2025. During the meeting 
there were a total of two public comments, and the public hearing was closed. The Planning 
Commission requested a site visit which was coordinated for October 28, 2025. Following the 
site visit, the Planning Commission reconvened for a brief work session where they provided 
consensus to bring the item back for possible action. Members of the Planning Commission 
expressed concern with the overall increase in traffic at buildout and intersections which may 
fail. Carl Miller, the Summit County Transportation Planning Director was available at the 
September 23, 2025 meeting and provided supporting information regarding how the traffic 
will behave in this neighborhood. Please also reference to the Summit County Transportation 
Planning memo in Exhibit C which touches on several criteria, including traffic, parking, 
connectivity, and transportation. Additionally, the applicant has provided a supplemental letter 
(Exhibit B) to address the Planning Commission’s previous comments. 
 
Staff has included in the Exhibit package the links to each meeting.

Snyderville Basin Development Code Section 10-2-18(F) Required Findings of Fact states: The 
county must find enough evidence to support the following conclusions to approve an MPD. In 
some cases, conditions of approval will be attached to the approval to ensure compliance with 
these findings. 
 
1. The MPD is designed to fit well into the natural terrain, minimize excessive site grading and 
protect, preserve, and enhance the level of quality of the surrounding area. 
2. The MPD makes suitable provisions for the protection, preservation, and enhancement of 
watercourses, drainage areas, wooded areas, rough terrain and similar natural features 
and areas. 
3. The MPD takes adjacent land uses into consideration and mitigates potential impacts, 
including but not limited to flooding, erosion, subsidence, sloping of the soil or other 
dangers and nuisances, through careful planning. 
4. The MPD has direct vehicular access from a state highway or county road or suitable 
private road or driveway access meeting all requirements of the county engineer and fire 
marshal. 
5. The MPD has a secondary point of access/emergency access or other mitigation 
satisfactory to the Summit County engineer and fire marshal. 
6. All roads/streets within MPD follow the natural contours of the site wherever possible to 
minimize the amount of grading. 
7. Existing or proposed utility and public services are adequate to support the proposed MPD 
at normal service levels and are designed in a manner to avoid adverse impacts on adjacent 
land uses, public services, and utility resources. 
8. The proposed structures within the MPD are located on the most developable and least 
environmentally sensitive portions of the site. The open areas within the MPD are designed 
so that existing significant vegetation can be maintained to the greatest degree possible. 
9. The MPD includes adequate internal vehicular and pedestrian/equestrian/bicycle 
circulation. 
10. The building/buildings within the development provide good quality internal and external 
environments for their users, promoting health and welfare. 
11. The proposed project integrates existing and incorporates new natural features into a 
multifunctional network that supports quality of place, biodiversity, and water 
management.  
12. The proposed project contributes to Summit County's sustainability goals, 
13. The MPD, as conditioned, complies with all the requirements of chapter 4 of the Snyderville 
Basin development code, 
14. The MPD, as conditioned, is consistent with the general plan. 
15. The MPD has been noticed and public hearing held in accordance with this code. (Ord. 913, 
6-9-2021)

General Plan Review 
 
All MPD applications shall be reviewed for consistency with the goals and objectives of the 
Snyderville Basin general plan; however, such review for consistency shall not alone be 
binding.  
 
The Future Land Use Map designates this property as a Mixed Use area. 
 
Chapter 2 (Land Use) of the Snyderville Basin General Plan starts with; 
 
“GOAL: Promote sustainable Land Use Planning Principles that preserve Critical Lands, maintain 
neighborhood character, protect the economic base, prevent sprawl, and provide efficient 
delivery of services. 
 
OBJECTIVE A: Encourage the creation of highly-livable neighborhoods and mixed use areas that 
are connected by open spaces and multi-modal transportation systems. 
 
Policy 2.1: Identify, plan, and create neighborhood master planned specific areas where 
development/redevelopment should occur. These designated areas should be rezoned 
accordingly. 
 
Policy 2.2: Identify specific areas where development should not occur and zone it 
accordingly. 
 
Policy 2.3: Do not approve any new entitlements beyond those permitted by the 
Development Code until such time that existing entitlements are significantly exhausted, 
unless the County legislative body first determines that: 
 
a. a compelling countervailing public interest, specifically identified in the General Plan 
exists and cannot be reasonably satisfied without expanding one or more entitlement(s); 
 
b. such new entitlement(s) do not simply result in an incidental benefit to the public 
interest, but rather such entitlement(s) are intended primarily to promote such 
compelling countervailing public interest; and 
 
c. any new entitlement(s) are consistent with the Neighborhood Planning Area Plans and 
the Snyderville Basin General Plan’s Future Land Use Maps, as amended. 
 
Policy 2.4: Work with local, state, and federal agencies to create a multi-modal transportation 
network to support desired development patterns. The Snyderville Basin will be defined by its 
abundant open spaces, world-class resort amenities, and livable neighborhoods and not an 
expansive highway system.” 
 
The proposed project is located in the Kimball Junction Neighborhood of the General Plan.  
The goals, objectives, and policies of the Snyderville Basin General Plan were further expanded 
in the Kimball Junction Neighborhood Plan.  
147
18 
 
 
Kimball Junction Neighborhood Plan Fundamental Planning and Community Design 
Principles: 
 
• Create a Mixed-Use Neighborhood states; Mixed-Use neighborhoods are designed 
with people in mind. They are “Places”. They are places where residential, retail, 
service, and civic uses are connected by a network of public spaces. Mixed-Use 
neighborhoods promote efficiencies in people, services, mobility, and infrastructure. 
 
Analysis: Junction Commons was developed as an auto-dependent retail center. The 
applicant is proposing to redevelop the project as a mixed-use development with 
significant portions of retail and commercial. The applicant is proposing internal and 
external multi-modal connectivity improvements. These improvements would result 
with better connectivity outside of the project and would connect the development 
to the rest of the mixed-use neighborhood. COMPLIES AS CONDITIONED 
 
• Create a People Oriented Built Environment states; Establishing a more traditional 
street and building environment will ensure priority is given to the needs of 
pedestrians rather than the movement of vehicles. Traditional streets are not 
dominated by large parking lots, blank building walls, service areas, and utility 
infrastructure. Creating buildings with a continuous active edge, with doors and 
windows opening onto the street, and parking located behind and/or underneath the 
buildings can help encourage comfortable pedestrian activity. The goal should be to 
accommodate vehicles in an environment built for people. 
 
Analysis: By virtue of design, the existing Junction Commons functions as an auto 
destination center. The development is dominated by two large surface parking lot 
areas with a pedestrian perimeter circulation route and retail spaces surrounding 
the parking lots. While the proposed redevelopment is expected to remain as 
primarily an auto destination center, the applicant has implemented design that 
significantly improves multi-modal transportation opportunities and people oriented 
built environment. While the applicant has not provided architectural details, the 
site layout provides clear pedestrian thoroughfare where pedestrians are prioritized 
ahead of vehicular traffic. The introduction of housing on site is expected to activate 
the area for pedestrians and encourage neighborhood uses rather than destination 
uses. COMPLIES AS CONDITIONED 
 
• Achieve a Seamlessly Connected Neighborhood states; Successful neighborhoods are 
designed to connect people to where they want to go in a simple, safe, comfortable, 
and enjoyable manner. 
 
Analysis: The applicant is proposing to redevelop the site with additional multi-
modal access points. Additionally, the applicant is proposing to expand the 
pedestrian and bicycle infrastructure that connects the project with the 
neighborhood. COMPLIES AS CONDITIONED   
 
148
19 
 
• Create a Walkable Neighborhood states; Human beings became bipedal nearly two 
million years ago. We are good walkers. Successful neighborhoods should be 
designed to reflect this skill. Walkable neighborhoods foster a diversity of people, 
uses, and experiences. They are easier to move to, move through, and move 
around. Mixed-Use neighborhoods create a greater sense of community. 
 
Analysis: The existing Junction Commons property was developed as a single-use 
destination retail center, relying predominantly on the use of a private automobile. 
The applicant is proposing additional pedestrian, bicycle, and transit infrastructure.  
COMPLIES AS CONDITIONED 
 
• Develop Centralized Parking Facilities states; Parking lot location is significant in 
influencing transportation habits, since almost all car trips start and end in a parking 
space. When parking is concentrated in an appropriately located facility, it frees 
space for more offices, restaurants, stores, parks, and landscaped areas for 
people to gather. 
 
Analysis: Currently there are two parking areas at Junction Commons. The lower and 
larger parking area includes a landscaped island with a playground that is the only 
available pedestrian route that does not involve walking through the parking lot 
directly. The island also includes an existing Summit County e-bike station. Most 
trips to Junction Commons start and end via the private automobile. The applicant is 
proposing to delete the landscaped island and to implement a pedestrian 
thoroughfare intermingled with mixed use development. The applicant is providing 
multimodal opportunities that previously did not exist at this location. The applicant 
is proposing multiple pedestrian and bicycle routes, multiple bicycle parking areas, 
multiple e-bike parking areas, a dedicated public transit slip lane, and EV 
parking/charging areas. The applicant is also proposing a parking structure that will 
be triggered in certain phases of the buildout. COMPLIES AS CONDITIONED 
 
• Provide a Variety of Housing Choices states; Housing Choice is a key component of a 
strong and healthy community. Kimball Junction housing will meet the diverse needs 
of the region in a variety of forms that responds to our local context. Providing a 
variety of housing options with a wide range of affordability gives people of all social 
and economic backgrounds the opportunity to live in a quality built environment. 
 
Analysis: The project includes a variety of apartments and townhomes with one, 
two, and three-bedroom units. The applicant has not provided detail as to the 
variety of Annual Median Income (AMI) pricing. The applicant will be required to 
provided further details as part of the Development Agreement process and final 
Housing Agreement. COMPLIES AS CONDITIONED AND FINALIZED IN THE 
DEVELOPMENT AND HOUSING AGREEMENTS  
 
• Make Visual Quality a Top Priority states; How a neighborhood looks affects how 
residents feel about where they live, work, and recreate. Creating a sense of place 
149
20 
 
greatly depends on the quality of the buildings and the spaces around them. 
Uncluttered and well-maintained areas that are designed for a variety of experiences 
will help create places that are lively and pleasant to use. 
 
Key themes could vary depending on the project scale, but would typically be 
structured around: 
• Building location, height, scale, and architecture. 
• Public realm, including public spaces and landscaping elements. 
• Ensuring that all details of a project are reviewed and taken into consideration 
upfront, not as an afterthought (e.g. mailbox locations, utility box locations, public 
furniture locations, and dumpster locations). 
• Enhance both legibility and understanding of the neighborhood and minimize 
clutter.  
• Consider how a climate responsive design would influence the physical nature of 
the built environment. 
 
Analysis: Please refer the NMU-1, MPD, and Chapter 4 analysis above. COMPLIES AS 
CONDITIONED 
 
• Create a Sustainable Community states; Sustainable Growth is more than promoting 
environmentally friendly policies and regulations; it is about making positive 
economic, environmental and social progress for this and future generations. 
 
Support Summit County’s transition to a low carbon future in a changing climate, 
encourage the reuse of existing resources, including adaptive reuse of existing 
buildings, and encourage the use of renewable resources. 
 
Promote the vitality of Kimball Junction’s main commercial spaces and protect the 
natural areas around them.  
 
Support a pattern of development which facilitates the use of a variety of sustainable 
modes of transportation.  
 
Locate developments that generate significant movement where the need to travel 
will be minimized and the use of sustainable transportation modes can be 
maximized. 
 
Contribute to building a strong, responsive and competitive economy, by ensuring 
that the Snyderville Basin General Plan and Development Code support appropriate 
growth and innovation. 
 
Support strong, vibrant and healthy communities, by providing the supply of housing 
required to meet the needs of present and future generations. 
 
Create a high quality built environment, with accessible local services that reflect the 
community’s needs and support its health, social, and cultural well-being. 
150
21 
 
 
Analysis: The Summit County Sustainability Division has stated that based on the 
information the applicant has provided, both in their MPD proposal and 
supplemental Sustainability Brief, as well as through speaking with the team, they 
believe there are adequate plans to meet the sustainability-related requirements for 
the Rezone. However, they still recommend that additional details be included in 
any Development Agreements that are generated if the Rezone is 
granted. COMPLIES AS CONDITIONED 
 
• Design for Change states; Development needs to be flexible enough to respond to 
future changes in demography and lifestyle. This means designing for energy and 
resource efficiency, creating flexibility in the uses of property, public spaces and  
service infrastructure, and introducing new approaches to transportation, traffic 
management and parking. 
 
Analysis: The median age of Summit County residents is 41.8 years and is the 
highest in Utah (average age in Utah is 31.3 years).  Most common type of home 
built in Summit County is 4+ bedrooms while 2-person households are the most 
dominant and 1-person households are the fastest growing. The median home sales 
price in Summit County is ~$1.3M.  The proposed redevelopment is implementing a 
variety of centrally located uses and is more aligned with the smaller type of 
households that are dominant in Summit County. The project location, clustered 
mixed-use design and variety of multi-modal opportunities support the future 
changes within Summit County. COMPLIES AS CONDITIONED 
     
• Neighborhood Engagement states; Successful plans must always include a quality 
neighborhood engagement effort. Plans fail when property owners, neighborhood 
residents and other important community stakeholders feel left out of the 
neighborhood planning and design implementation process. 
 
Analysis: Staff is unaware of any efforts being made by the applicant to actively 
engage public outside the required planning process.

Site Design Narrative 
 
An MPD application shall include a written explanation of how the project plan addresses the 
following design questions: 
 
• Project Neighborhood Connectivity 
The applicant has stated that;  
“(1) Vehicles will enter the site from the three existing locations, two along Landmark 
Drive and one from the intersection at Powderwood Drive and Overland Drive.  
(2) The interior of the project will develop new vehicular and pedestrian circulation 
patterns, but not new streets as defined by code. (3) New access points through the 
site are proposed from Powderwood Drive, which services Crestview and 
Powderwood residential properties, and Landmark Drive which provides improved 
access from the North and East of the property. Improved connections to existing 
trails are proposed at all areas where they approach the site, improving access to the 
Millennium Trail, the trail to Walmart, access to AC Hotel, and Canyon Corner 
development. The commuter trails provide improved access to Pinebrook, Bring 
Creek, and Kimball Junction.” 
 
• Availability of Neighborhood Public Facilities 
 
The applicant has stated that; “The site is situated adjacent to retail centers and public 
open space. It is within reasonable distance to public facilities such as libraries, schools, 
and transit centers. A new transit stop and new bike-share facilities are also proposed as 
part of this development. The proposed development also adds a mix and diversity of 
uses that create a neighborhood within the property.” 
 
• Housing Needs 
 
The applicant has stated that; “The proposed project provides multiple levels of housing 
that include townhomes, studio apartments, 1- bedroom units, 2-bedroom units, and 3-
bedroom units. Both the market rate units and affordable housing units will provide 
needed housing for the community.” 
 
• Character 
 
152
23 
 
The applicant has stated that; “The intent of the design is to create a place for people 
that helps to convert the haphazard development on the west side of Kimball Junction 
into a community with a center, improving the properties around the project as much as 
the project itself. The proposed design changes the nature of a strip mall surrounding a 
large parking lot into an active retail and mixed-use development through site planning, 
scale, landscape, and placement of uses.” 
 
• Site Design 
 
The applicant has stated that; “The proposed design follows the existing topography, 
provides landscaped areas where a large parking lot currently exists, provides street 
trees that help define the public spaces, provides much desired shade in the summer, 
maintains a significant portion of the existing structures, and minimizes grading 
throughout the site.” 
 
• Complete Street Design 
 
The applicant has stated that; “The project employs multiple vehicular and pedestrian 
pathways that provide safer use of the existing site while creating public spaces.” 
 
• Parking Areas 
 
The applicant has stated that; “One of the primary goals of the project was to break up 
the existing massive parking lots. The proposal removes the upper lot and breaks the 
lower lot into 5 smaller lots. Improvements are also created at the perimeter of the 
property to supplement the use for employees and overflow use. The revisions reduce 
the visibility of the parking lots, reduce the need for significant re-grading, and improve 
stormwater run-off by compartmentalizing and reducing the surface parking area.” 
 
• Public and Private Outdoor Spaces 
 
While the applicant has not commented on Public and Private Outdoor Spaces, these 
spaces are being provided throughout the project.  
 
• External Storage and Service Facilities 
 
The applicant has stated that; “Areas throughout the site are designated for garbage 
collection and recycling. Mail will be coordinated with the Postmaster. Delivery areas will 
remain in place for existing commercial, while the new commercial has been designed to 
provide drop-off locations at each Pod.” 
 
• Building Design 
 
The applicant has stated that; “The design of the commercial building pods (5-7) has 
been created to help define public space. The design also allows the public areas to open  
153
24 
 
 
up to the south, which improves performance during the winter. The basic building 
design for the residential creates space between buildings that improves light and air 
access. It also allows easy access to trails and open space. The building depths support 
quality unit design that is not too deep, such that each unit will have excellent access to 
the exterior. The locations for the residential provide a separation from the primary 
commercial areas while still providing easy access to pedestrians and bicyclists.” 
 
During the October 28, 2025 discussion, the Planning Commission directed Staff to return the 
application for a possible action. COMPLIES AS CONDITIONED 
 
Development Agreement 
  
Per Snyderville Basin Development Code MPD Section 10-2-18(D.4); Once the County Council 
approves a Master Planned Development, the approval shall be put in the form of a

REZONE 
 
Per Snyderville Basin Development Code MPD Section 10-7-4; Approval of an amendment to the 
zone district shall not be granted until both the commission and county council have reviewed 
the specific development proposal and determined: 
 
(1) The amendment complies with the goals, objectives and policies of the general plan, the 
neighborhood planning area plan, and the 
land use plan maps; 
 
(2) The amendment is compatible with adjacent land uses and will not be overly burdensome on 
the local community; 
 
(3) The specific development plan is in compliance with all applicable standards and criteria for 
approval as described in chapters 3 and 4 of this title; and 
 
(4) The amendment does not adversely affect the public health, safety and general welfare. 
 
Analysis: All rezones, projects with more than 10 units of density, and/or commercial uses over 
10,000 sq. ft. are required to be processed through the MPD process. The NMU-1 and MPD 
review were created to be a more intensive review of projects. Please refer to the code analysis 
in sections above for further details. COMPLIES AS CONDITIONED

Public Notice, Meetings and Comments 
 
The Planning Commission held a public hearing on September 23, 2025. During the meeting 
there were a total of two public comments, and the public hearing was closed.  
 
This item has been scheduled as a possible action by the Planning Commission.  
 
As of the date of this report, no public comment has been received. 
 
Recommendation 
 
Snyderville Basin Development Code Section 10-2-18(D.3) MPD Process states;  
 
b. Planning Commission Review And Public Hearing: Except in the case of MPDs that 
include a rezone, the planning commission shall approve, approve with modifications, or deny 
an MPD. The planning commission action shall be in the form of written findings of fact, 
conclusions of law, and in the case of approval, conditions of approval. Action shall occur only 
after public hearing is held. To approve an MPD, the planning commission will be required to 
make the findings outlined in section F herein. In the case of MPDs that include a rezone, the 
planning commission shall review and forward a recommendation to the county council, 
including findings of fact, conclusions of law and conditions of approval. 
 
c. County Council Review And Public Hearing: In the case of MPDs that include a rezone, 
the county council shall approve, approve with modifications, or deny an MPD. The county 
council action shall be in the form of written findings of fact, conclusions of law and in the case 
of approval, conditions of approval. Action shall occur only after a public hearing is held. To 
approve an MPD, the county council will be required to make the findings outlined in section F 
herein. 
 
Staff recommend that the Snyderville Basin Planning Commission review and discuss the 
proposed Junction Commons Master Planned. If the Snyderville Basin Planning Commission is in 
a position to make a positive recommendation, Staff has included below draft Findings of Fact, 
Conclusions of Law and Conditions of Approval for consideration. The Planning Commission 
may add further Conditions of Approval.  
 
Alternatively, the Planning Commission has the option to request additional information and/or 
articulate their position to direct Staff to return to a future meeting with a set of Findings of 
Fact and Conclusions of Law to support their alternative recommendation to the Summit 
County Council.  
 
Findings of Fact 
 
1. Outlets Park City, formerly Tanger Outlets and the Factory Stores at Park City, 
(“Outlets”) was originally built in 1991 with 208,669 square feet through the Class II 
Permit process under a previous version of the Snyderville Basin Development Code.  
155
26 
 
 
2. The 1990 Class II Permit review and approval was based on infrastructure and design 
criteria. 
3. The 1990 Class II Permit for the original portion of the development did not include an 
underlying development agreement.  
4. In 1999 the Summit County Board of County Commissioners approved a 92,567 square 
foot addition to the Outlets pursuant to the Factory Stores Park City Specially Planned 
Area and Development Agreement, Ordinance 368 (“1999 DA”).  
5. Community benefits in the 1999 DA included a Snyderville Basin Special Recreation 
District trail improvement, seating areas within the site, and preservation of existing 
parks and open space areas.  
6. The 1999 DA allowed unlimited Restaurant uses. 
7. The 1999 DA expired in October 2004. 
8. In 2014, due to the expiration of the 1999 DA, the Summit County Council approved a 
SPA designation, Ordinance 828, for a 23,500 square foot retail addition through the 
Tanger Outlets Specially Planned Area and Development Agreement (“2014 DA”).  
9. The public benefit required in the 2014 DA was a financial contribution to the Peace 
House project.  
10. The 2014 DA expired in August of 2024. 
11. According to the Summit County Assessor’s Office, the total square footage of the 
Outlets is currently 322,804 square feet.   
12. The applicant is requesting a Master Planned Development (MPD) for the Junction 
Commons, a 19-building mixed use redevelopment project located at 6699 N Landmark 
Dr (Parcel FSE-1), Kimball Junction, Summit County, UT.  The applicant is proposing to 
Rezone Parcel FSE-1 from the Town Center Zone to the Neighborhood Mixed-Use 1 
(NMU-1) Zone. 
13. The project includes the redevelopment of the upper portion of the property into a 
residential project while the lower portion of the property would be developed with 
additional infill development.  
14. The upper portion of the property is currently developed with ~98,000 sq. ft. of retail 
space. The applicant is proposing to demolish ~82,000 sq. ft. of the existing retail and 
replace it with a 10-building residential project which includes 433 units (391 
apartments and 42 townhomes) and a ~7,200 sq. ft. amenity/clubhouse space within 
one of the three apartment buildings. One of the buildings is proposed to be mixed use 
and would contain ~7,300 sq. ft. commercial. The applicant is also proposing two 
parking structures in the upper portion of the property. 
15. The lower portion of the property is currently developed with ~230,000 sq. ft. of retail 
space. The applicant is proposing to infill the parking area with a 9-building mixed-use 
project with ground floor commercial (~54,000 sq. ft.) and upper floor residential (77 
apartments).  
16. The Planning Commission held meetings regarding the proposal on April 9, 2024, August 
27, 2024, February 11, 2025, March 25, 2025, and October 28, 2025. 
  
17. The Snyderville Basin Planning Commission has reviewed the proposal for compliance 
with applicable Development Code standards. 
18. Service providers have reviewed the proposal for compliance with applicable standards. 
 
 
156
27 
 
 
Conclusions of Law 
 
1. The proposed rezone, as conditioned, meets the applicable standards set forth in the 
Snyderville Basin Development Code, Section 10-2-17: Neighborhood Mixed-Use 1 
(NMU-1) Zone. 
2. The proposed rezone, as conditioned, meets the applicable standards set forth in the 
Snyderville Basin Development Code, Section 10-2-18: Master Planned Developments 
(MPD). 
3. The proposed rezone, as conditioned, meets the applicable standards set forth in the 
Snyderville Basin Development Code, Section 10-7-4: Amendment to the Zoning Map. 
 
Conditions of Approval 
 
1. All necessary land use application approvals must be obtained prior to the Building 
Permit submittal. 
2. The applicant as part of the Development Agreement shall provide detailed drawings 
and design criteria which demonstrate full compliance with the Snyderville Basin 
Development Code. 
3. The applicant as part of the Development Agreement shall provide a detailed 
breakdown of market rate and affordable units. 
4. The applicant as part of the Development Agreement shall include a provision that in 
case commercial uses become more intense than outlined that the applicant/owner of the 
property is responsible to pay the difference via a fee-in-lieu.  
5. Summit County Staff shall review and approve a Construction Mitigation plan prior to 
final approval of the Development Agreement.  
6. The applicant shall provide a detailed  grading and retaining wall plan prior to final 
approval of the  Development Agreement.  
7. The applicant as part of the Development Agreement shall provide a detailed 
landscaping plan to be adopted as part of the Development Agreement. 
8. The applicant as part of the Development Agreement shall provide a detailed lighting 
plan to be adopted as part of the Development Agreement. 
9. The applicant as part of the Development Agreement shall provide a draft Community 
Benefits Plan to the Summit County Council which outlines all community benefits, 
when they are triggered, and by when they must be provided.  
10. The applicant as part of the Development Agreement shall include a more detailed plan 
and a contingency requirement that any snow outside of the 71 parking stalls be 
removed off site at the developer’s expense within 24 hours. 
11. Summit County Transportation Planning Division has included the following condition; 
 
Applicant shall fund and construct two Summit Bike Share stations (12 docks / 8 bikes 
each): 
o One near the upper roundabout/transit stop 
o One near Landmark Dr./Millennium Trail connection 
o Stations to meet Summit Bike Share design standards (access, power, site prep) 
o Stations to be completed in Phase 3 prior to new building permits.

12. All Mail Delivery requirements shall be met per the Park City Post Master. 
13. All service provider requirements shall be met. 
14. All other criteria and conditions contained in this Staff Report shall be included into the 
required Development Agreement.

JUNCTION COMMONS 
Master Planned Development - Summit County, Utah
August 1, 2025
SUSTAINABILITY BRIEF 
EXHIBIT A.67
225
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
The Junction Commons Project represents a transformative redevelopment of 37.3 acres in the 
Kimball Junction area of Summit County, Utah. This Master Planned Development (MPD) with 
Neighborhood Mixed-Use 1 (NMU-1) zoning demonstrates comprehensive sustainability 
through environmental stewardship, social equity, and economic viability. The project converts 
an existing single-use outlet mall and oversized parking areas into a mixed-use, pedestrian-
focused, transit-oriented community that aligns with Summit County's sustainability objectives 
and the Snyderville Basin General Plan.
PROJECT OVERVIEW 
• Location: Kimball Junction, Summit County, Utah
• Total Area: 37.3 acres
• Zoning: NMU-1 (Neighborhood Mixed-Use 1) with MPD overlay
• Development Type: Redevelopment/Mixed-use
• Housing Units: 432 units (market rate and affordable)
• Commercial Space: Reduction from 318,986 SF to 301,360 SF
• Green Space: 10 acres (26.8% of property)
• Pedestrian Plazas: 4.2 acres (11.2% of property)
SUSTAINABILITY FRAMEWORK 
1. ENVIRONMENTAL SUSTAINABILITY 
1.1 Site Restoration and Land Use
• Brownﬁeld Redevelopment: Converting existing asphalt parking lots and underutilized 
commercial structures
• Minimal Site Disturbance: Utilizing previously disturbed land to minimize 
environmental impact
• Native Habitat Restoration: Restoring native grasses and vegetation where asphalt 
currently exists
• Biodiversity Enhancement: Landscaping will provide biodiversity where none 
currently exists
1.2 Water Conservation and Quality
• Xeriscaping Implementation: Drought-tolerant and native plant species throughout the 
development
• Smart Irrigation Systems: Time-controlled watering and sensor-based irrigation systems
• Stormwater Management: Upgrading existing stormwater systems and reducing 
impermeable surface area
• Water Quality Protection: Located on existing asphalt, minimizing risk to surrounding 
waterways
• Groundwater Protection: Converting asphalt parking lots reduces groundwater 
contamination risk
EXHIBIT A.68
226
1.3 Air Quality Improvement
• Increased Vegetation: Breaking up existing heat sink of parking lots with plant life
• Transportation Alternatives: Encouraging walking, biking, and public transit over 
single-occupant vehicles
• Mixed-Use Design: Reducing vehicle trips through integrated live-work-play 
environment
• Carbon Footprint Reduction: Higher sustainability standards for new construction
1.4 Energy Efﬁciency and Renewable Energy
• Solar-Ready Infrastructure: All new buildings designed for solar panel installation
• Photovoltaic (PV) Solar Infrastructure: Two parking structures with solar panel 
installation
• Grid-Tied Renewable Energy: Solar installations connected to utility grid
• Passive Solar Design: Building orientation optimized for winter solar gain for outdoor 
gathering spaces
• Natural Gas Provision: Mixed-Use buildings will be supplied with Natural Gas to the 
retail portions to provide efﬁcient options for high temperature water needs and cooking
• Natural Ventilation: Building design incorporating wind-driven ventilation strategies
• Daylighting Optimization: Building depths supporting excellent natural light access
• Energy-Efﬁcient Building Envelope: Detailed R-values and MEP systems meeting 
efﬁciency standards (see “Proposed Sustainable Speciﬁcations”)
1.5 Sustainable Materials and Construction
• Sustainable Building Materials: Consideration of environmental impact in material 
selection as per the “Sustainable Material Selection Framework” for the project
• Waste Minimization: Maintaining signiﬁcant portion of existing structures
• Construction Waste Management: Comprehensive recycling and waste disposal 
agreements (see “Proposed Sustainable Speciﬁcations”)
• Resource Conservation: Minimizing excavation and material import/export (see 
“Proposed Sustainable Speciﬁcations”)
2. SOCIAL SUSTAINABILITY 
2.1 Affordable Housing Integration
• Workforce Housing: Affordable housing units integrated throughout the development
• Diverse Housing Types: Studio apartments, 1-bedroom, 2-bedroom, and 3-bedroom 
units
• Simultaneous Development: Affordable housing built with each development phase
• Accessibility Compliance: ADA-compliant units and facilities throughout
2.2 Community Connectivity and Accessibility
• Universal Design: Full ADA compliance including accessible EV charging stations
• Multi-Generational Spaces: Housing and amenities for young families, young adults, 
and elderly
EXHIBIT A.69
227
• Public Realm Enhancement: Converting parking lots into community gathering spaces
• Cultural Preservation: Design respecting mountain resort community character
2.3 Health and Wellness
• Active Transportation: Enhanced pedestrian and bicycle infrastructure
• Trail Connections: New internal trails connecting to Millennium Trail system
• Public Open Space: 26.8% of property dedicated to green space
• Air Quality Improvement: Reduced vehicle dependency improving local air quality
• Community Social Interaction: Plazas, gathering spaces, and amphitheater facilities
3. ECONOMIC SUSTAINABILITY 
3.1 Transit-Oriented Development
• Enhanced Transit Access: New transit stop with improved amenities
• Reduced Transportation Costs: Mixed-use design reducing need for vehicle ownership
• Summit Bike Share Integration: On-site bike share stations reducing transportation 
expenses
• E-bike Infrastructure: Charging stations throughout the development
3.2 Economic Diversiﬁcation
• Mixed-Use Integration: Commercial, residential, ofﬁce, and restaurant uses
• Live-Work Opportunities: Ground-ﬂoor commercial with residential above
• Local Business Support: Spaces designed for neighborhood-serving retail and services
• Employment Generation: New commercial spaces creating job opportunities
3.3 Infrastructure Efﬁciency
• Parking Optimization: Right-sizing parking supply to reduce overbuilt infrastructure
• Shared Parking Analysis: Efﬁcient use of parking resources between residential and 
commercial uses
• EV Charging: Charging stations provided in each phase including EV ready spaces 
• Utility Coordination: Working with existing utility providers to optimize service 
delivery
• Phased Development: Eight-phase approach allowing for market-responsive 
development
COMPLIANCE WITH SUMMIT COUNTY REQUIREMENTS

• Bike storage and multi-modal transportation options
• Compliance with Snyderville Basin Special Recreation District Trail Design Standards

SUSTAINABILITY METRICS AND TARGETS

buildings

• Renewable Energy: Solar-ready infrastructure for future 100% renewable energy 
potential

EXHIBIT A.72

• Local Employment: New commercial spaces supporting local job creation

IMPLEMENTATION STRATEGY 
Phase 1: Infrastructure and Site Preparation
• Utility coordination and upgrades
• Stormwater system improvements
• Initial landscaping and native restoration
• Transit stop enhancement
Phase 2-8: Incremental Development
• Building construction with sustainability features
• Progressive parking lot conversion to green space
• Trail system development and connections
• Community amenity installation
Monitoring and Evaluation
• Annual sustainability performance reporting
• Water and energy consumption tracking
• Transportation mode split analysis
• Community satisfaction and health indicator monitoring
CONCLUSION 
The Junction Commons Project represents a model of sustainable development that transforms 
an underutilized commercial site into a vibrant, environmentally responsible, and socially 
equitable mixed-use community. Through comprehensive environmental management, social 
inclusion, and economic viability strategies, the project aligns with Summit County's 
sustainability objectives while creating a replicable framework for similar redevelopment 
initiatives.
The project's commitment to environmental stewardship through native landscape restoration, 
energy efﬁciency, renewable energy infrastructure, and water conservation, combined with its 
dedication to social sustainability through affordable housing, accessibility, and community 
EXHIBIT A.73
231
connectivity, positions Junction Commons as a signiﬁcant contribution to Summit County's 
sustainable development goals.
By prioritizing transit-oriented development, active transportation, and mixed-use design, the 
project reduces environmental impact while enhancing quality of life for residents and the 
broader Kimball Junction community. The phased implementation approach ensures 
sustainable growth management while allowing for adaptive responses to market conditions 
and community needs.
