SUSTAINABLE MATERIAL SELECTION FRAMEWORK 
EXHIBIT A.75
233
Framework Overview 
This comprehensive framework evaluates exterior building materials across four critical 
sustainability dimensions while ensuring compatibility with Park City's modern mountain 
aesthetic and climate requirements.
Evaluation Criteria & Scoring Matrix 
1. Environmental Impact Assessment (25 points) 
Carbon Footprint Analysis
Embodied Carbon (10 points)\
• 9-10 points: <50 kg CO2e/m² (reclaimed wood, recycled metal)
• 7-8 points: 50-150 kg CO2e/m² (FSC wood, natural stone)
• 5-6 points: 150-300 kg CO2e/m² (ﬁber cement, engineered wood, stucco)
• 3-4 points: 300-500 kg CO2e/m² (brick, conventional concrete)
• 1-2 points: >500 kg CO2e/m² (aluminum, steel, vinyl, synthetic stucco)
Material Source & Transportation (8 points)
• 7-8 points: Local materials (<200 miles) or rapidly renewable
• 5-6 points: Regional materials (200-500 miles)
• 3-4 points: National materials (500-1500 miles)
• 1-2 points: International materials (>1500 miles)
End-of-Life Impact (7 points)
• 6-7 points: Fully recyclable or biodegradable
• 4-5 points: Partially recyclable (>50%)
• 2-3 points: Limited recyclability (20-50%)
• 1 point: Non-recyclable or hazardous disposal
2. Durability & Maintenance Requirements (25 points) 
Lifespan Expectancy (12 points)
• 11-12 points: 75+ years (natural stone, quality metal)
• 9-10 points: 50-75 years (ﬁber cement, engineered wood)
• 7-8 points: 30-50 years (quality wood siding, stucco, synthetic stucco)
• 5-6 points: 20-30 years (vinyl, composite)
• 1-4 points: <20 years (poor quality materials)
Maintenance Frequency (8 points)
• 7-8 points: Minimal maintenance (<5 year intervals)
• 5-6 points: Low maintenance (5-10 year intervals)
• 3-4 points: Moderate maintenance (3-5 year intervals)
• 1-2 points: High maintenance (<3 year intervals)
Climate Resilience (5 points)
• 5 points: Excellent performance in freeze-thaw, UV , moisture
• 4 points: Good performance with minor seasonal effects
EXHIBIT A.76
234
• 3 points: Adequate with standard weatherization
• 2 points: Requires enhanced protection systems
• 1 point: Poor climate performance
3. Energy Performance (25 points) 
Thermal Performance (12 points)
• 11-12 points: R-value >20, minimal thermal bridging
• 9-10 points: R-value 15-20, low thermal bridging
• 7-8 points: R-value 10-15, moderate thermal bridging
• 5-6 points: R-value 5-10, some thermal bridging
• 1-4 points: R-value <5, signiﬁcant thermal bridging
Solar Reﬂectance & Heat Island Effect (8 points)
• 7-8 points: Solar reﬂectance >0.6, cool material properties
• 5-6 points: Solar reﬂectance 0.4-0.6
• 3-4 points: Solar reﬂectance 0.2-0.4
• 1-2 points: Solar reﬂectance <0.2, heat absorption
Air Sealing Properties (5 points)
• 5 points: Excellent air barrier properties
• 4 points: Good air sealing with proper installation
• 3 points: Moderate, requires additional air barrier
• 2 points: Poor air sealing properties
• 1 point: Allows signiﬁcant air inﬁltration
4. Modern Mountain Aesthetic Compatibility (25 points) 
Design Versatility (10 points)
• 9-10 points: Excellent color/texture options, design ﬂexibility
• 7-8 points: Good variety, moderate design options
• 5-6 points: Limited but adequate options
• 3-4 points: Few design choices
• 1-2 points: Very limited aesthetic options
Architectural Integration (10 points)
• 9-10 points: Perfect ﬁt for modern mountain style
• 7-8 points: Excellent compatibility
• 5-6 points: Good ﬁt with some adaptation
• 3-4 points: Requires signiﬁcant design accommodation
• 1-2 points: Poor aesthetic ﬁt
Aging Characteristics (5 points)
• 5 points: Ages beautifully, develops attractive patina
• 4 points: Ages well with minimal aesthetic degradation
• 3 points: Neutral aging, maintains appearance
• 2 points: Shows wear but acceptable
• 1 point: Ages poorly, requires frequent refresh
EXHIBIT A.77
235
Material Categories & Recommended Applications 
Primary Structure Materials (Score Range: 80-100) 
Natural Stone Veneer
• Best for: Foundation, accent walls, mixed-use common areas
• Strengths: Durability, local sourcing potential, thermal mass
• Considerations: Higher embodied carbon, installation complexity
Reclaimed/FSC Wood Siding
• Best for: Residential portions, accent features
• Strengths: Carbon sequestration, aesthetic appeal, local availability
• Considerations: Maintenance requirements, ﬁre treatment needs
Cor-Ten Steel Panels
• Best for: Commercial facades, structural accents
• Strengths: Durability, modern aesthetic, self-protecting
• Considerations: Initial cost, detailing requirements
Fiber Cement Siding
• Best for: Large residential facades, cost-conscious areas
• Strengths: Low maintenance, ﬁre resistance, design ﬂexibility
• Considerations: Embodied carbon, transportation distance
Secondary/Accent Materials (Score Range: 65-79) 
Cor-Ten Steel Panels
• Best for: Commercial facades, structural accents
• Strengths: Durability, modern aesthetic, self-protecting
• Considerations: Initial cost, detailing requirements
Engineered Wood Products
• Best for: Residential siding, sofﬁt applications
• Strengths: Consistent quality, modern proﬁles available
• Considerations: Moisture management, end-of-life disposal
•
Supporting Materials (Score Range: 50-64) 
High-Performance Stucco Systems (synthetic stucco)
• Best for: Continuous insulation applications
• Strengths: Seamless appearance, integrated insulation
• Considerations: Climate performance, color retention
Pre-ﬁnished Steel Panels
• Best for: Continuous insulation applications
• Strengths: Durability, modern aesthetic, self-protecting
• Considerations: Initial cost, color retention
EXHIBIT A.78
236
Implementation Guidelines 
Phase 1: Site Analysis 
Conduct detailed assessment of:
• Local material availability and suppliers
• Speciﬁc microclimate conditions
• Existing architectural context
• Municipal design guidelines and restrictions
Phase 2: Material Selection Process 
1. Preliminary Screening: Remove materials scoring <50 points
2. Detailed Analysis: Calculate total lifecycle costs for top performers
3. Design Integration: Test aesthetic combinations and transitions
4. Performance Modeling: Verify energy performance assumptions
Phase 3: Speciﬁcation Development 
• Develop detailed installation speciﬁcations
• Establish quality control protocols
• Create maintenance schedules and protocols
• Document warranty and performance expectations
Performance Monitoring & Optimization 
Success Metrics
• Annual energy consumption targets
• Maintenance cost tracking
• Aesthetic satisfaction surveys
• Environmental impact veriﬁcation
Summit County Speciﬁc Considerations 
Climate Factors
• Extreme temperature variations (-20°F to 90°F)
• High UV exposure at elevation (7,000+ feet)
• Signiﬁcant freeze-thaw cycles
• Heavy snow loads and ice formation
Regulatory Environment
• Consider ﬁre safety guidelines for wildland-urban interface
• Energy code compliance (current and anticipated future)
• Water conservation and runoff management
EXHIBIT A.79
237
Local Resources
• Prioritize Utah and Colorado sourced materials
• Consider partnerships with regional manufacturers
• Evaluate reclaimed material availability from local projects
• Assess workforce expertise for specialized installations
This framework provides a systematic approach to achieving sustainable, durable, and 
aesthetically appropriate exterior material selection while supporting long-term building 
performance and community values.
General Note:
This point system and scoring framework is based on general knowledge of sustainable building practices, 
material properties, and industry standards, but doesn’t reference speciﬁc published frameworks or 
standards.
The framework draws conceptually from several established systems and principles:
• LEED (Leadership in Energy and Environmental Design) - particularly the Materials and 
Resources credits
• Living Building Challenge materials requirements
• BREEAM (Building Research Establishment Environmental Assessment Method) material 
evaluation approaches
• Embodied carbon assessment methodologies from organizations like the Carbon Leadership 
Forum
• Durability and maintenance standards from ASTM and other technical organizations
• Energy performance metrics from building codes and green building standards
However, the speciﬁc point distributions (like the 25-point weighting for each category, or the exact 
ranges for embodied carbon) are a synthesis rather than being pulled from a single authoritative source.

238

PROPOSED SUSTAINABLE SPECIFICATIONS 
EXHIBIT A.81

SECTION 017419 - CONSTRUCTION WASTE MANAGEMENT AND DISPOSAL 
1.GENERAL
1. SECTION REQUIREMENTS
A. Action Submittals:
1. Waste Management Plan: Submit plan within 30 days of date established for 
commencement of the Work.
B. Informational Submittals:
1. Waste Reduction Progress Reports: Submit concurrent with each Application for 
Payment. Include total quantity of waste, total quantity of waste salvaged and 
recycled, and percentage of total waste salvaged and recycled.
2. Records of Donations and Sales: Receipts for salvageable waste donated or sold 
to individuals and organizations. . Indicate whether organization is tax exempt.
3. Recycling and Processing Facility Records: Manifests, weight tickets, receipts, 
and invoices.
4. Landﬁll and Incinerator Disposal Records: Manifests, weight tickets, receipts, 
and invoices.
5. Statement of Refrigerant Recovery: Signed by refrigerant recovery technician 
responsible for recovering refrigerant, stating that all refrigerant that was present 
was recovered and that recovery was performed according to EPA regulations.
C. Refrigerant Recovery Technician Quali ﬁ cations: Certi ﬁ ed by EPA-approved 
certiﬁcation program.
D. Waste Management Conference: Conduct conference at Project site to comply with 
requirements in Section  013000 "Administrative Requirements." Review methods and 
procedures related to waste management.
E. Waste Management Plan: Develop a waste management plan consisting of waste 
identiﬁcation, waste reduction work plan, and cost/revenue analysis. Indicate 
quantities by weight or volume, but use same units of measure throughout waste 
management plan.
1. Salvaged Materials for Reuse: Identify materials that will be salvaged and 
reused.
2. Salvaged Materials for Sale: Identify materials that will be sold to individuals 
and organizations, include list of their names, addresses, and telephone numbers.
3. Salvaged Materials for Donation: Identify materials that will be donated to 
individuals and organizations, include list of their names, addresses, and 
telephone numbers.
4. Recycled Materials: Include list of local receivers and processors and type of 
recycled materials each will accept. Include names, addresses, and telephone 
numbers.
5. Cost/Revenue Analysis: Indicate total cost of waste disposal as if there was no 
waste management plan and net additional cost or net savings resulting from 
implementing waste management plan.
EXHIBIT A.82
240
2. PRODUCTS
1. PERFORMANCE REQUIREMENTS
A. Achieve end-of-Project rates for salvage/recycling of [50] [75] <Insert number> 
percent by weight of total nonhazardous solid waste generated by the Work.
3. EXECUTION
1. PLAN IMPLEMENTATION
A. General: Implement approved waste management plan. Provide handling, containers, 
storage, signage, transportation, and other items as required to implement waste 
management plan during the entire duration of the Contract.
B. Training: Train workers, subcontractors, and suppliers on proper waste management 
procedures, as appropriate for the Work occurring at Project site.
1. Distribute waste management plan to entities when they ﬁrst begin work on-site. 
Review plan procedures and locations established for salvage, recycling, and 
disposal.
2. SALVAGING DEMOLITION WASTE
A. Salvaged Items for Reuse in the Work: Clean salvaged items and install salvaged items 
to comply with installation requirements for new materials and equipment.
B. Salvaged Items for Sale and Donation: Permitted on Project site.
C. Salvaged Items for Owner's Use: Clean salvaged items and store in a secure area until 
delivery to Owner.
D. Doors and Hardware: Brace open end of door frames. Except for removing door 
closers, leave door hardware attached to doors.
E. Equipment: Drain tanks, piping, and ﬁxtures. Seal openings with caps or plugs.
F. Plumbing Fixtures: Separate by type and size.
G. Lighting Fixtures: Separate lamps by type and protect from breakage.
3. RECYCLING WASTE
A. General: Recycle paper and beverage containers used by on-site workers.
B. Packaging:
1. Cardboard and Boxes: Break down packaging into ﬂat sheets. Bundle and store 
in a dry location.
2. Polystyrene Packaging: Separate and bag materials.
3. Pallets: As much as possible, require deliveries using pallets to remove pallets 
from Project site. For pallets that remain on-site, break down pallets into 
component wood pieces and comply with requirements for recycling wood.
4. Crates: Break down crates into component wood pieces and comply with 
requirements for recycling wood.
C. Asphaltic Concrete Paving: Grind asphalt to maximum 4-inch (100-mm) size.
D. Asphaltic Concrete Paving: Break up and transport paving to asphalt-recycling facility.
E. Concrete: Remove reinforcement and other metals from concrete and sort with other 
metals.
1. Pulverize concrete to maximum 4-inch (100-mm) size.
F. Masonry: Remove metal reinforcement, anchors, and ties from masonry where feasible 
and sort with other metals.
EXHIBIT A.83
241
1. Pulverize masonry to maximum 4-inch (100-mm) size.
2. Clean and stack undamaged, whole masonry units on wood pallets.
G. Wood Materials:
1. Sort and stack reusable members according to size, type, and length. Separate 
lumber, engineered wood products, panel products, and treated wood materials.
2. Clean Cut-Offs of Lumber: Grind or chip into small pieces.
3. Clean Sawdust: Bag sawdust that does not contain painted or treated wood.
H. Metals: Separate metals by type.
I. Asphalt Shingle Rooﬁng: Remove and dispose of nails, staples, and accessories.
J. Gypsum Board: Stack large clean pieces on wood pallets or in container and store in a 
dry location.
K. Acoustical Ceiling Panels and Tile: Stack large clean pieces on wood pallets and store 
in a dry location.
L. Metal Suspension System: Separate metal members including trim, and other metals 
from acoustical panels and tile and sort with other metals.  Prepare for recycling.
M. Carpet and Pad: Roll large pieces tightly after removing debris, trash, adhesive, and 
tack strips.
1. Store clean, dry carpet and pad in a closed container or trailer provided by 
Carpet Reclamation Agency or carpet recycler.
N. Piping: Reduce piping to straight lengths and store by type and size. Separate 
supports, hangers, valves, sprinklers, and other components by type and size.

SECTION 018113.36 - SUSTAINABLE DESIGN REQUIREMENTS 
PART 1 - GENERAL 
1.1 SECTION REQUIREMENTS 
A. Deﬁnitions: 
1. Regional Materials: Materials that have been extracted, harvested, or recovered, as well as 
manufactured, within 500 miles (800 km) of Project site. If only a fraction of a product or 
material is extracted, harvested, or recovered and manufactured locally, then only that 
percentage (by weight) shall contribute to the regional value.  
2. Recycled Content: The recycled content shall be determined by weight. 
• "Postconsumer" material is deﬁned as waste material that is generated by end users 
of the product and that can no longer be used for its intended purpose.  
• "Preconsumer" material is deﬁned as material diverted from the waste stream 
during the manufacturing process. Excluded is reutilization of materials such as 
scrap generated in a process that is reclaimed in the same process that generated it.  
B. Submittals: 
1. Project Materials Cost Data: Provide statement indicating total cost for materials used for 
Project. Costs exclude labor, overhead, and proﬁt. Include breakout of costs for furniture, 
plumbing, mechanical, electrical, elevators and equipment and wood-based 
construction materials. Submit within 30 days of date established for commencement of 
the Work.  
2. List of proposed materials with recycled content. Indicate postconsumer recycled content 
and preconsumer recycled content for each product. Submit within 30 days of date 
established for commencement of the Work.  
3. List of proposed regional materials. Submit within 30 days of date established for 
commencement of the Work.  
4. Product Data.

3.2 CONSTRUCTION INDOOR-AIR-QUALITY MANAGEMENT 
A. Comply with SMACNA's "IAQ Guideline for Occupied Buildings under Construction." 
1. If Owner authorizes use of permanent heating, cooling, and ventilating systems during 
construction period as speciﬁed in Section 015000 "Temporary Facilities and Controls," 
install ﬁlter media having a MERV 8 according to ASHRAE 52.2 at each return-air inlet for 
the air-handling system used during construction.  
2. Replace all air ﬁlters immediately prior to occupancy.  
B. After construction ends, prior to occupancy and with all interior ﬁnishes installed, perform a 
building ﬂush out by supplying a total volume of 14000 cu. ft. (4 300 000 L) of outdoor air per 
square foot (square meter) of ﬂoor area while maintaining an internal temperature of at least 60 
deg F (16 deg C) and a relative humidity no higher than 60 percent. 
EXHIBIT A.88
246
C. After construction ends, prior to occupancy and with all interior ﬁnishes installed, perform a 
building ﬂush out by supplying a total volume of 3500 cu. ft. (1 070 000 L) of outdoor air per 
square foot (sq. m) of ﬂoor area to the space. Once a space is occupied, ventilate it at a minimum 
rate of 0.30 cfm per sq. ft. (1.52 L/s per sq. m) of outside air. During each day of the occupied 
ﬂush-out period, ventilation shall begin a minimum of three hours prior to occupancy and 
continue during occupancy. Maintain these conditions until a total of 14000 cu. ft./sq. ft. (4 300 
000 L/sq. m) of outside air has been delivered to the space.

SECTION 08561 – VINYL WINDOWS

PART 1 – GENERAL
1.01 DEFINITIONS
A. U-Factor - Btu/hr .  sq.ft . *F: Lower value means higher insulation value.
B. R-Value - Inverse of U-value: Resistance to heat ﬂow. Higher value means higher insula-
C. Center-of-Glass Performance Values: U-factor or R-value calculated at center of glass (no 
“Edge effect”)
D. Unit Performance Values: U-factor or R-value calculated for entire glazing area (NFRC 
certiﬁed values based on standard-sized glazing units)
E. Visible Transmittance - Tois: % of light energy transmitted by given glazing
F. Solar Heat Gain Coefﬁcient (SHGC): Measure of total solar heat transmitted by given 
glazing
G. Shading Coefﬁcient: Solar heat transmitted relative to a single glass pane
H. Luminous Efﬁcacy Factor - (ke) = Tvis/SC: Ratio of light transmission divided by 
shading coefﬁcient
I. Solar Reﬂectance: % of light or solar energy that a given glazing reﬂects to the outside
J. Tinted Glass: Controls the amount of sunlight admitted
K. Spectrally Selective Glazings: Glazings that selectively transmit sunlight of particular 
wavelength to minimize unwanted heat gain while having minimal impact on visible 
light transmission
EXHIBIT A.99
257
1.02 SUBMITTALS

Rating Council (NFRC) 100, measuring total U-factor for a window averaged over the

Association (SIGMA)-approved certiﬁcation program.

replace units which fail to maintain hermetic seal of air space, deteriorate, or otherwise

RE: Supplemental information in response to October 28 meeting 
 
Dear Planning Commissioners:  
 
This law firm represents Junction Commons (or the “Applicant”) formerly known as “The 
Tanger Outlets at Park City” and as “The Factory Stores at Park City.” Please accept this letter as 
a formal supplement to our prior submissions and a response to questions raised by the Planning 
Commission in prior hearings.   
 
Junction Commons is proposing a rezone from the Town Center zone to the Neighborhood 
Mixed Use (“NMU”) zone. In conjunction with that rezone (and as required by Summit County 
Code) Junction Commons is proposing a master planned development (“MPD”). The rezone and 
MPD will facilitate redevelopment of the existing outlet center as a mixed-use development. In 
prior work sessions and public hearings, Junction Commons established that its proposal meets the 
requirements for a rezone to NMU and the criteria necessary for the adoption of an MPD.  
 
A majority of the Planning Commissioners indicated in the most recent hearing that they’re 
prepared to move forward with a vote on Junction Commons’ applications. The Commission 
instructed Staff to schedule a vote and prepare draft conditions of approval, findings of fact, and 
conclusions of law supporting approval as soon as practicable. We appreciate the Commissions 
careful consideration of our application and willingness to move forward.  
 
There are a few items identified as discussion items in Staffs’ thorough report. The Planning 
Commissioners provided input on those discussion items and appear, generally, to be in support 
EXHIBIT B.1

Junction Commons 
November 18, 2025

of Junction Commons’ request. However, to further support that decision, the Applicant provides 
the following supplemental information on those points of discussion.  
 
1. Is the building height exception up to 60 feet merited and should it include an additional 
exception for mechanical equipment.  
 
The regulation of building height in the NMU zone is found in Snyderville Basin Development 
Code Section 10-2-17(F). That section provides that “the maximum building height in the NMU-
1 Zone shall be forty-five feet (45’).” However, the Code also provides an exception, stating: “The 
County Council may grant a height exception up to a maximum of 60 feet to accommodate 
affordable housing and/or structured parking through the MPD process.” Id.  
 
The Junction Commons MPD proposes 205 units of affordable housing disbursed throughout 
the proposed redevelopment and features structured parking for residential and commercial shared 
uses, thereby satisfying both conditions for a height exception. The Commission expressed some 
reservations about the increase to 60 feet in building height and for that reason requested a site 
walk with a story pole demonstrating the proposed height. In response to that request, the Applicant 
installed a pole at 60 feet and noted other existing buildings at the property that are at, and near, 
60 feet in overall building height.  
 
 
EXHIBIT B.2
266
Junction Commons 
November 18, 2025 
 
3 
Junction Commons was originally constructed in a former quarry. As such, the existing grade 
is well below that of nearby properties (Walmart and Liberty Peak). The design team located the 
proposed new buildings in this depression, while respecting the adjoining Liberty Peak apartments 
massing by interposing a townhome product as a transition in scale. As shown in the following 
images (and highlighted by the red dashed line), the buildings requesting the exception are well 
below surrounding uses. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Put simply, given topography, massing, and scale of the surrounding uses the building height 
exception in this instance will have minimal impact on adjoining properties.  
 
Staff also raised questions regarding the potential placement of mechanical equipment and 
elevator penthouses on the roof of the Applicant’s proposed 60-foot buildings and questioned 
whether Building Height should be measured from the top of this equipment. In response, the 
Applicant indicated that it believes mechanical equipment should be exempt from the Building 
Height measurement. However, in the event the Planning Commission and County Council intend 
that the max 60-feet Building Height include mechanical equipment, the Applicant is willing to 
abide by that height limitation as well by locating mechanical elsewhere—this is reflected in the 
Applicants’ submittal drawings.

EXHIBIT B.3

Junction Commons

The foregoing images illustrate that the project’s proposed buildings include a parapet at the 
perimeter of the building form. This parapet is under the sixty-foot building height requirement 
and the elevator overruns work within the height of the parapet. By specifying an elevator 
manufacturer whose equipment requires only a 4-foot shaft extension above the roof level, the 
elevator machinery remains concealed behind the parapet and within the 60-foot height limit. The 
EXHIBIT B.4
268
Junction Commons 
November 18, 2025 
 
5 
elevator cab will serve all occupied floors, with the machine room and equipment housed in this 
minimal overhead space that does not require a visible penthouse structure. 
 
Furthermore, any roof access stair has been redesigned as a partially exterior stair, with 
approximately half of the upper run exposed to the elements. A code-compliant landing with 
integrated drainage has been incorporated at the transition point, eliminating the need for a fully 
enclosed stair tower or penthouse. While this open-air configuration creates operational 
challenges, it meets building code requirements for roof access while avoiding additional building 
height. 
 
The original penthouses projected above the primary roofline as separate enclosed structures, 
contributing to the overall building height. The revised approach tucks all mechanical and access 
elements within or flush with the roof plane, concealed behind the building parapet. This design 
change reduces visual massing while maintaining full functionality and code compliance. 
 
So, even if no building height exception is allowed for mechanical equipment, the Applicant 
will comply with the 60-foot height limitation. A height that is appropriate for the location and 
proposed uses.  
 
2. The Applicant is proposing to construct its affordable housing requirement as the first 
product in each phase of its development and requests the Planning Commission’s support 
for a reduction in its affordable housing obligation in return.  
 
The Applicant is seeking a reduction in its affordable housing obligation in exchange for 
building the affordable housing as the first product in each phase of its redevelopment. This is 
expressly allowed under the code. Snyderville Basin Development Code Section 10-5-4(F)(1) 
states that “[i]f a developer provides all the required affordable housing up front (prior to the first 
certificate of occupancy for the market portion of the development), the number of required AUEs 
may be reduced  by up to twenty five percent (25%) at the discretion of the land use authority.”  
 
There was some question as to whether it is appropriate to apply the reduction where the 
affordable housing is being constructed in phases. The Applicant is constructing the affordable 
housing per phase because that is required by the code. Snyderville Basin Development Code 
Section 10-5-3(4) provides that “[t]he affordable housing component in a development shall be 
constructed concurrently with the rest of the development” and “[e]ach phase of a project must 
contain a proportionate amount of the required affordable housing.” If each phase is required to 
“contain a proportionate amount of the required affordable housing”, then it is the only way to 
front-load and meet the requirements of Chapter 5.  
 
Here, the Applicant is proposing to construct the affordable housing first in each phase of its 
development, prior to the certificate of occupancy being granted for any market portion of that 
phase. The first phase of the residential redevelopment of the upper lot will begin with the 
construction of an all-affordable building containing 97 deed restricted affordable units with a mix 
EXHIBIT B.5
269
Junction Commons 
November 18, 2025 
 
6 
of studio, one-bedroom, and two-bedroom apartments. This all-affordable building will be the first 
new building constructed as part of this development.  
 
Each subsequent phase of the proposed redevelopment supplies the proportionate share of 
affordable units as the first units delivered to qualify for the bonus reduction in the affordable 
housing requirements contemplated in the code. For the phases of redevelopment occurring in the 
lower lot, the affordable housing will be provided in the form of residential units constructed as 
upper floors above ground floor commercial offerings. It bears noting that all residential product 
constructed in the lower lot will be affordable, there is no market rate residential product 
contemplated as part of those phases.  
 
The layers in yellow (the upper stories) are the residential component of the in-fill development 
of the lower lot. All of this residential will be affordable units:

For each of these buildings, the affordable residential units will be completed first, with no 
certificates of occupancy granted on the market rate components until after the certificates of 
occupancy are obtained for the affordable residential.  
 
This redevelopment proposes to replace approximately 100,000 square feet of existing outlet 
retail space and associated parking with mixed-income housing units, including the 204 deed 
restricted affordable units. In other words, the Applicant is proposing to replace existing 
commercial density with, among other uses, affordable housing units. This results in a net positive 
benefit to the community because it provides affordable housing in place of existing commercial 
uses.  
 
As discussed in the prior public hearing, the reduction is an incentive built into the code to 
motivate developers to build affordable housing first, not last. Here, we’re committed to building 
that housing first. In exchange, we’re requesting a total reduction in our affordable housing 
obligation of approximately 16%.  
 
Staff also raised a question regarding the affordability of the townhome product in phase four 
of the proposed redevelopment. The townhome product proposed is a for-sale product. Phase four 
includes 30 units of affordable deed restricted apartments in addition to the for-sale townhomes. 
The Applicant is meeting its affordable obligation without including the townhome product and 
there is nothing in the code implying that an applicant is required to include affordable product 
amongst every distinct housing type. That said, the Applicant is willing to restrict two of its for-
sale townhome units to be sold to an attainable buyer at 120% of Summit County AMIs. To be 
clear, this is not an obligation under the code. Rather, it is an additional gesture of goodwill that 
the Applicant is willing to provide as part of this redevelopment.  
 
3. The Applicant will mitigate any construction traffic harm and is proposing increasing 
pedestrian connectivity and multi-modal options that result in a net positive in overall 
transportation solutions in the area.  
 
As with all development in Summit County, traffic impacts are always a concern. This is true 
during construction phases, after occupancy, and into the future. The Applicant’s traffic study 
demonstrates that its proposal will not increase the failure of any intersection in the area beyond 
background failures occurring as a result of existing conditions.  
 
Planning Commissioners asked questions regarding impacts to surrounding property owners 
that may result due to construction traffic. Any time you engage in redevelopment in a town center 
such as Kimball Junction impacts on existing property owners are a concern. Here, the Applicant 
is willing to agree to conditions of approval that mitigate against harms that may result to 
neighboring property owners as a result of construction traffic. During the permitting process, it is 
anticipated that the Applicant and its contractor will be required to provide a Construction

Mitigation Plan that must include a detailed Traffic Control Plan. That plan is required to include 
conditions aimed at minimizing community and environmental impacts during construction.2  
 
The Applicant is proposing to replace existing commercial uses with a mix of uses including 
residential that will increase the walkability of the site. To facilitate this pedestrian access the 
Applicant is proposing a wholesale improvement to the internal and external trail connections for 
the site. The Applicant is also proposing to incorporate a bike route to and through the site along 
with related bike parking conveniently located in improved park amenities.  
 
Consistent with the requirements of the NMU, the Applicant is also improving transit options 
to the site by providing a dedicated and improved transit stop with an improved shelter, waiting 
area, proximate restrooms, bicycle lockers, and bike racks.

4. The site is designed to take advantage of the topography and maximize utility through 
redevelopment of an existing commercial development.  
 
The design concept takes advantage of the topography by locating the tallest sections of the 
project in an area that is lower than the surrounding properties. By locating residential in this area, 
adjacent to existing residential it works as a buffer to the commercial property above to the east 
and below to the north. Additionally, the project has been designed to transition in height from the 
existing residential buildings located on the west of the property with smaller scale townhouses 
transitioning to the taller residential. 
 
The design also maximizes utility by removing the commercial from the southern area of the 
site and concentrating commercial in the center of the existing expansive parking lot. This move 
EXHIBIT B.16
280
Junction Commons 
November 18, 2025 
 
17 
creates public spaces for pedestrians at the heart of the commercial development and encourages 
pedestrian access over the current car-centric design.

Memo 
To:        Snyderville Planning and Zoning Commission 
From:     Carl Miller, PMP, AICP CTP, Transportation Planning Director 
Date:       September 5, 2025 
Subject:  Junction Commons Master Planned Development (24-087) 
 
Overview 
 
The Summit County Transportation Planning Division has reviewed the Junction Commons 
Master Planned Development located at 6699 N Landmark Dr (Parcel FSE-1) and would like to 
address the following items for the benefit of the Snyderville Planning and Zoning Commission, 
including the Traffic Impact Study, parking, and site design based on the August 6, 2025 plans 
submitted. 
 
The project proposes: 
• 432 new residential units 
• 51,000 sq. ft. of new commercial space 
• Demolition of ~97,505 sq. ft. of commercial space 
• Total future commercial: ~287,000 sq. ft. 
 
Key transportation review topics: 
1. Traffic Impact Study 
2. Parking and Phasing 
3. Site Circulation and Transit 
 
Traffic Impact Study 
The county Automobile Level of Service (LOS) standards are “roadways and intersections in the 
Snyderville Basin shall be a level of service C for county roads and intersections.” The applicant 
hired Hales Engineering to conduct a Traffic Impact Study (TIS), dated May 2, 2025. The TIS 
evaluated traffic generated by the project and the capacity for existing roads and intersections 
for existing conditions (2024) and future (2029 and 2050) conditions, with and without trips 
generated by the development. The counts were performed on Wednesday, May 15 and 
Thursday, May 16, 2024. Here is a summary of the findings: 
EXHIBIT C.1
283
Key Findings 
• Daily trips generated: 3,184 
• Peak hour trips: +242 AM, +578 PM (net increase) 
• Existing LOS: 
o Whole Foods/Landmark Dr → LOS D (degrades to LOS F) 
o Walmart/Landmark Dr → LOS D (degrades to LOS F) 
• Safety: No fatal/serious injury crashes in past 5 years 
• Access: East access to Landmark Dr. restricted to right-turn egress only 
Study Limitations 
• May traffic counts are 13% below peak July/December volumes. Consultant noted that 
adjusting for seasonality would not significantly change recommendations. 
Recommended Improvements (from TIS) 
• Roundabout at Landmark Dr./Whole Foods intersection (ROW feasibility TBD) 
• Widening westbound Walmart/Landmark Dr. approach for turn lanes, or restricting to 
right-turn only 
Transportation Planning Recommendations – Traffic Impact Study 
• Traffic counts were performed during May 15-16, 2024. The May counts do not reflect 
peak seasonality and are 13% less than summer (July) and winter (December) peaks. The 
applicant should update each figure that reflects May volume to reflect peak season 
volumes (July or December) so this can be confirmed. 
• Off-site improvements, such as an improvement at the Whole Foods/Landmark Drive 
intersection and for the westbound approach at Walmart and Landmark Drive will be 
led by the County at the appropriate time. Transportation impact fees levied to this 
development may help fund these improvements. 
• east access from Junction Commons onto Landmark Drive will create an additional 
egress movement approximately 200’ NW of the Canyon Corner/Landmark Drive 
intersection. This location is near a curvature of Landmark Drive. This access provides an 
additional location for egress and is in very close proximity to the main bicycle and 
pedestrian access from Landmark Drive. While the TIS indicates this should be “stop-
controlled and allow for egress movements only.” The applicant has agreed to restrict 
this as a stop-controlled, right-out egress movements only to improve safety and traﬃc 
ﬂow at this location.  
Parking and Phasing 
The applicant is requesting a parking exception based on principles of shared use, demand 
management, and efficient land use. The proposal’s mixed-use design further supports the 
EXHIBIT C.2
284
exception request by enabling shared parking. Residential, commercial, restaurant, office, and 
retail uses typically generate parking demand at different times of the day and week, reducing 
conflicts and allowing spaces to serve multiple purposes. This “right-sizing” of parking better 
reflects actual demand while maintaining neighborhood character. 
To support the request, the applicant conducted a parking analysis showing that 1,504 spaces 
are sufficient. The analysis, detailed in the “Parking Adjustments” section (pp. 50–52), identifies 
peak parking demand as follows: 
Requirement 
 
Spaces 
 
Notes 
 
Code required 1,651 Based on standard rates 
Shared parking demand 1,331 Peak December weekend 
Proposed supply 1,504 720 surface, 784 structured 
When compared against requirements and projected demand, the proposed supply 
demonstrates sufficiency.  
Parking Phases 
Providing sufficient parking at each phase is also critical to ensuring traffic efficiency and safety. 
The applicant has proposed construction over 7 phases, with additional development and 
parking coming in Phases 3-7. Each phase of the project provides suﬃcient parking to meet 
demand, with spaces located in close proximity to the new developments. This approach avoids 
the creation of unnecessarily large surface lots and distributes parking more evenly across the 
site. 
Snow Storage 
The proposal includes a “Snow Storage Plan” as reflected on p. 12. Snow storage could occupy 
up to 71 stalls temporarily during peak snow season. As July and December are the peak 
parking months, this should rarely conflict with peak parking demands. Staff recommends that 
the applicant relocate any snow storage occurring outside of these designated parking stalls 
shall be off-site at the developer’s expense within 48 hours.  
These findings confirm that the proposed parking supply not only exceeds peak demand but 
also reflects a more efficient, context-sensitive approach to parking provision (Exhibit A.37). 
Transportation Planning Recommendations – Parking and Phasing: 
The following will help ensure that parking is effective and efficient for the development: 
• Provide EV parking distributed throughout the development, approved by the county 
Sustainability team, to include:  
o 10% of total stalls with charging stations  
EXHIBIT C.3
285
o 20% EV-ready (wired for future conversion), as proposed 
• Provide snow storage and snow storage overflow parking as proposed in the “Snow 
Storage Plan” exhibit. Any snow storage occurring outside of these parking stalls shall be 
relocated off-site within 24 hours at the developer’s expense. 
 
Site Circulation and Transit 
The Snyderville Basin Development Code requires that all Master Planned Developments in the 
NMU-1 zone provide a transit center, transportation stops, and/or other improvements 
necessary to mitigate the impacts of the development, “unless otherwise permitted through 
the Master Planned Development process.” The Code further specifies that transit centers shall 
include a building with a waiting area, restrooms, bicycle lockers, and bike racks, while all 
transportation stops must include shelters and bicycle racks. 
Proposal 
• Bus stops are sited in near the roundabout between the upper and lower site areas and 
near the Millennium Pathway along Landmark Drive. 
• Bus stops include waiting area, bike racks, and Summit Bike Share stations. 
• No restrooms are provided as High Valley Transit has determined that nearby publicly 
accessible restrooms in the development are sufficient. 
• Wayfinding signage plan (p. 7) will guide riders to amenities. 
• All residential buildings to include secure indoor bike storage. 
The proposed bus stops and nearby amenities meet the intent of the Transit Center 
requirement. Although restrooms are not included within the facility, High Valley Transit has 
confirmed this is acceptable due to the availability of privately-owned, publicly accessible 
restrooms within the development. The Transit Center’s central location provides convenient 
access for both new on-site residents and surrounding neighborhoods. In addition to bike racks, 
the facility will include a Summit Bike Share station to strengthen multimodal connectivity. All 
residential buildings will provide secure indoor bicycle storage, and public restrooms are 
available nearby as part of neighborhood improvements. To further support access, the 
applicant has submitted a wayfinding signage plan (“Site Circulation,” p. 7) to help transit riders 
easily locate these amenities in compliance with NMU-1 Code requirements. 
Transportation Planning Recommendations – Circulation/Transit 
• The applicant shall provide 45-degree parking with a minimum of 33’ of total parking 
and backup/service drive area between the wheel stop. This will help reduce vehicular 
and bicycle conflicts. This will also need to be approved by the Fire District prior to final 
approvals. 
• Deliveries shall occur at front entrances during off-peak hours to minimize conflicts. 
EXHIBIT C.4
286
• Commission/staff shall review wayfinding signage and phasing during Phase 3, prior to 
new building permits. 
• Applicant shall fund and construct two Summit Bike Share stations (12 docks / 8 bikes 
each): 
o One near the upper roundabout/transit stop 
o One near Landmark Dr./Millennium Trail connection 
o Stations to meet Summit Bike Share design standards (access, power, site prep) 
o Stations to be completed in Phase 3 prior to new building permits 
EXHIBIT C.5
287
